## [TinyCeNN-LM converts pretrained attention into cellular-recurrent layers with quality gating](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21139) ⭐️ 7.0/10

TinyCeNN-LM introduces a quality-gated post-training conversion framework that replaces attention in pretrained language models with CeNN-inspired cellular-recurrent layers, using bounded local processing, compact recurrent memory, routing, fusion, and accept-or-rollback validation. On SmolLM2-135M, layers 0-2 are accepted with cumulative ΔNLL of +0.01209 while layer 3 is rejected for failing representation fidelity, and on Qwen3.5-0.8B full-attention layers 3, 7, and 11 are accepted with final ΔNLL of +0.02073. This work addresses a key compatibility problem in model conversion—replacing attention without breaking representations expected by later layers—and shows that conservative, quality-gated structural conversion is more reliable than universal attention replacement or claimed speedups. It is likely to interest researchers in efficient NLP, model compression, and inference optimization for small language models. Integrated Memory keeps perplexity within -0.07% to +0.93% while reducing total cache by up to 6.01%, and a sampled 200-item downstream sanity check gives 28.5%-32.0% overall accuracy for converted Qwen releases. The strict PDelta3 conversion accepts a layer only when both representation and NLL criteria pass fixed thresholds, illustrating that some layers may be rejected even when NLL looks acceptable.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Transformer language models rely on attention layers to mix information across tokens, and replacing them after pretraining is difficult because later layers expect specific representations. Cellular Neural Networks (CeNNs) are inspired by locally connected cellular processing, and here they motivate recurrent layers with bounded local computation and compact memory. Quality-gated conversion means each candidate layer is validated against representation-fidelity and negative log-likelihood (NLL) thresholds, with rollback if it fails.

## [InterviewPlayground: Clinician-Grounded QA Platform for AI Psychiatric Intake](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21149) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces InterviewPlayground, a clinician-grounded evaluation platform built around a memory-augmented patient simulator for open-ended AI interviewing. In a pilot with 6 clinicians over a 25-minute assessment, a GPT-based LLM intake interviewer recovered 88.0% of clinically relevant items versus 38.9% for clinicians, but made more unsupported clinical inferences (56.8% vs. 27.8%) and characterized identified safety concerns less often (33.3% vs. 66.7%). As health systems move toward deploying AI-assisted psychiatric intake, they need practical, low-burden ways to routinely check these tools against their own clinical quality standards. This platform offers a repeatable framework for comparing different interviewing approaches and measuring clinically relevant performance in a high-stakes setting, which could shape how responsible AI adoption is governed in mental health care. The platform combines repeatable simulated patients for cross-interviewer comparison, a low-burden clinician intake workflow, and form- and transcript-based evaluation, using expert-authored vignettes to embed clinically relevant items. The pilot's small size (6 clinicians, 25 minutes) and the finding that the LLM made more unsupported inferences highlight that higher recall does not necessarily mean better clinical judgment or safety handling.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Psychiatric intake is the initial interview in which a clinician gathers a patient's history, symptoms, medications, and safety concerns before treatment begins. A memory-augmented patient simulator is an AI-driven virtual patient that retains context across an interview, allowing open-ended questioning to be tested consistently; clinician-grounded evaluation means the assessment criteria and benchmarks are defined by practicing clinicians rather than purely automated metrics.

## [LLM Agents Design Chips via Higher-Level Abstraction, 2.6x Speedup](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21157) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.21157) compares four LLM-agent chip design flows—Direct RTL Design, Agent-based HLS Design, Post-Compiler HLS Refinement, and Post-HLS RTL Refinement—and introduces Agent-based HLS with RTL Refinement (AHRR), which combines agent-based HLS design with post-HLS RTL refinement. Across an 11-task benchmark suite evaluated on FPGAs, AHRR achieves a 2.6x geometric-mean speedup over Direct RTL Design, with code and artifacts released at github.com/ZijD/AHRR. Most existing LLM-for-chip-design work operates directly at RTL, so this paper's systematic comparison of abstraction levels offers evidence that higher-level abstractions can make agentic hardware design substantially more efficient. If validated, the AHRR workflow could shift how AI agents are applied in EDA, potentially lowering the effort needed to turn specifications into working FPGA or ASIC designs. The study uses FPGAs as a practical, easy-to-deploy evaluation platform but notes that the design-flow tradeoffs studied are largely independent of the target technology. Case studies indicate that HLS distills design knowledge into abstractions agents can exploit, while RTL refinement recovers lower-level optimization opportunities that HLS alone may miss.

**Background**: High-level synthesis (HLS) automatically converts an abstract behavioral specification, often written in C-like code, into register-transfer level (RTL) hardware descriptions, whereas RTL design manually specifies register operations and datapath logic at a lower level of abstraction. FPGAs are reconfigurable chips that let designers deploy and test hardware designs without fabricating silicon, making them a convenient end-to-end evaluation platform for comparing design flows. LLM agents are increasingly being explored as tools that can write, refine, and optimize such hardware descriptions.

## [SpecOpt: Agentic AI Framework Improves Drug Binding Specificity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21165) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced SpecOpt, a new molecular design task and agentic framework that makes constrained structural modifications to existing drugs to improve their binding preference for intended targets over known off-targets. On a ChEMBL-derived benchmark of 915 compounds, the agent improved the target-off-target binding gap for 84.8% of compounds, shifting the mean gap from -0.72 to +0.47 kcal/mol while maintaining a mean Tanimoto similarity of 0.72 to the starting compounds. Off-target binding is a major source of adverse drug effects, yet most structure-based design methods focus on generating selective compounds from scratch rather than fixing existing, well-characterized drugs. SpecOpt reframes specificity improvement as a distinct optimization problem and shows that residue-aware differential docking signals can guide an LLM to make targeted edits, which could offer a faster, cheaper route to de-risking known drugs. The framework docks each compound against its intended target and off-targets, compares poses through residue-aware atom-protein contacts, and feeds these differential interactions to an LLM to propose structural modifications; candidates are retained only if they satisfy molecular similarity, ADMET, and docking selectivity criteria. Ablation studies show that replacing residue identities with binary contact indicators eliminated improvement on all 29 ablation compounds, identifying residue-specific contact information as the critical optimization signal.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Molecular docking is a computational method that predicts the preferred orientation and binding affinity of a small molecule ligand to a protein target, and it is widely used in structure-based drug design. Binding specificity refers to a compound's ability to distinguish its intended target from other possible binding partners, called off-targets, and poor specificity can cause toxicity or side effects. ChEMBL is a manually curated database of bioactive molecules with drug-like properties that provides compound-target interaction data, which the authors used to build their benchmark.

## [Embed-TTT: Two-Step Test-Time Training Improves Rule Induction in ARC Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21181) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Embed-TTT, a two-step test-time training protocol that first finetunes only the task embedding and then freezes it to finetune the backbone, applied to the VARC model. Across ARC-AGI-1, ConceptARC, and two controlled datasets, Embed-TTT consistently yields task embeddings that better align with underlying rules, improve embedding-based retrieval, and enable accurate linear probing of known rules. This work addresses a key gap in ARC-like evaluations by separating rule induction from rule execution, which could lead to benchmarks that better distinguish in-distribution from out-of-distribution rules. It also shows that optimizing less than 0.01% of model parameters already solves a non-trivial fraction of ARC tasks, highlighting the potential of task embeddings for efficient reasoning. Embed-TTT recovers the underlying geometric structure of parametric rules and learns compositional capabilities enabling rule-wise interpolation, but not extrapolation. The full two-step pipeline improves final performance over optimizing only task embeddings, though the method is a focused contribution rather than a paradigm shift.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: The Abstraction and Reasoning Corpus (ARC), introduced by François Chollet in 2019, is a benchmark designed to measure AI skill acquisition and progress toward human-level AI through novel reasoning tasks. Test-time training (TTT) is a paradigm where model parameters update during inference for each individual prompt, allowing on-the-fly specialization and addressing distribution shift. Task embeddings are low-dimensional representations trained to capture task-specific information, and VARC is a model combining a pre-trained backbone with a trainable embedding representing the transformation rule.

## [CoVer: Information-Gain Rewards and Diversity-Pruned Tests for Code RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21208) ⭐️ 7.0/10

CoVer introduces a single-policy GRPO framework that co-trains one language model as both coder and test author, using an information-gain reward that scores each self-generated test by the mutual information between its pass/fail vector and a ground-truth-anchored correctness signal, plus a three-stage diversity-aware pruning step. On five benchmarks (LiveBench, MBPP, LiveCodeBench, CodeContests, CodeForces), it raises one-shot pass@1 by +5.8 points at 7B and +7.1 points at 14B over the Qwen2.5-Instruct backbone, and adds +3.5 points as a drop-in backbone inside the CodeT ranking pipeline. Self-play code RL has been limited by degenerate tests that reward trivial solutions, so a reward that explicitly measures how discriminative a test is could make co-training reliable at scale. Because CoVer improves both generation and selection, it points toward a practical recipe for building stronger code models without ever-expanding human-written test suites. The IG reward is gated by the sign of the covariance between a test's pass/fail vector and the graded correctness signal, so only positively discriminative tests receive reward; the three-stage pruning filters candidates by invalidity, input-string, and execution-profile redundancy to raise the effective sample size of the IG estimator at a fixed execution budget. Results are reported on a single backbone family (Qwen2.5-Instruct) at 7B and 14B, and the paper is an arXiv preprint without peer review or released community discussion.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) is a reinforcement learning algorithm that estimates advantages by normalizing rewards within groups of sampled candidates, avoiding the need for a separate value critic. In code-generation RL, models are typically trained against fixed test suites, but self-play methods instead let the model write its own tests, which risks permissiveness collapse (trivial tests that everything passes) and concentration bias (tests clustering on common inputs). CoVer targets both failure modes by rewarding tests for the information they carry about true correctness and by pruning redundant tests.

The method improves success rate from 59% with frozen perception to 83% in a perceptual-uncertainty ablation, and gradients from the planning objective can update perception parameters. ## [PlaceReasoner-Beta: Reasoning-Driven Macro Placement and Benchmarking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21263) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PlaceReasoner-Beta introduces a verifier-guided multi-agent framework that reformulates VLSI macro placement as a closed-loop reasoning problem, using a vision-language model planner, a geometric verifier, a physical verifier, and a post-route optimizer. The paper also releases PlaceReasoner-Bench, an open end-to-end benchmark of 8 designs at two aspect ratios (16 tasks) evaluated with routed PPA and DRC, where the method reduces post-route TNS by 61.2% at 1:1 and 53.0% at 2:1 versus the classical baseline. Macro placement strongly influences chip performance, power, and area, yet most existing methods optimize hand-crafted proxy objectives in a one-shot manner. By closing the loop with visual layout context, codified design expertise, and downstream physical-design feedback, this work points toward reasoning-based placement that could improve end-to-end layout quality and offers a reproducible benchmark for fair comparison. The framework never explicitly optimizes routed wirelength, yet it shortens routed wirelength on most designs, and it achieves the best timing among DRC-clean methods on all square tasks. As a beta version with no community discussion or engagement metrics yet, its impact remains to be validated by independent reproduction.

## [Production LLM Agent Benchmarking: Fixed Subsets Beat Adaptive Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21267) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A production deployment study of an analytics LLM agent serving tens of thousands of monthly active users compared four sampling strategies for recurring benchmarking across 574 historical runs split into calibration and held-out periods. Multidimensional 2PL adaptive testing achieved the best score fidelity, requiring only 200 questions (38.5% of a full run) for 1.03 percentage points of MAE, yet the team deployed difficulty-stratified fixed subsets for their operational simplicity. This is one of the few first-hand deployment studies of efficient recurring evaluation for production LLM agents, offering practitioners concrete guidance on balancing evaluation cost against score fidelity. The finding that difficulty-stratified fixed subsets transfer without recalibration to five other agent families and remain stable across calibration windows as short as one day makes the approach broadly applicable to teams running continuous agent evaluation. The study used 574 historical runs of the production benchmark, split chronologically into calibration and held-out periods, and compared random sampling, historical caching, fixed representative subsets, and IRT-based adaptive testing. While multidimensional 2PL adaptive testing delivered the best fidelity at 38.5% of full-run cost, the authors chose difficulty-stratified fixed subsets for operational simplicity, noting the contribution is an incremental methodological improvement rather than a paradigm shift.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Production LLM agents are evaluated repeatedly as they evolve, but rerunning full agent benchmarks is costly. Item Response Theory (IRT) is a psychometric framework that models the probability of a correct response as a function of item difficulty and discrimination; the two-parameter logistic (2PL) model is a common variant, and multidimensional IRT extends it to multiple latent abilities. Difficulty-stratified fixed subsets instead select a fixed set of questions spanning difficulty levels, avoiding the complexity of adaptive item selection.

A new arXiv preprint introduces GameASG-Bench, a benchmark that makes behavioral testability part of the autonomous software generation task for game development. It defines an evaluation interface specification before generation and implements 47 browser-native game-generation tasks across 12 primary genres and both 2D and 3D interaction, each with executable checks and an independently verified reference implementation. The benchmark exposes task-level compliance gaps that high average check pass rates obscure, showing that even the best agent stack achieved only a 55.3% strict task success rate despite a 93.2% mean L2 check pass rate. This matters because it gives the AI and software engineering community a rigorous way to measure whether autonomous agents actually satisfy behavioral requirements, not just produce runnable code. The protocol includes static L1 checks for source-level compliance and browser-executed L2 checks that combine semantic observations with real input and runtime evidence. Experiments across nine agent stacks found that for DeepSeek-V4-Flash, full tool access and larger nominal turn budgets yield more strict task successes, while strict task success rate is not monotonic in reasoning effort; both tested harnesses achieved 18 strict task successes but only ten tasks succeeded under both.

A new arXiv preprint introduces DENSE (Distilling Evidence from Nested Subtask Executions), a method that organizes online agent execution traces into evidence-grounded nested shortcut trees, compressing redundant attempts and reconciling issues across levels without needing post-hoc outcome labels. The paper also proposes REFIT, a source-paired evaluation protocol, and reports that DENSE achieves the highest strict pass rate among tested non-privileged feedback methods on Terminal-Bench 2.1 across four recipient models. On Terminal-Bench 2.1, DENSE improves strict pass rate by 7.12–15.64 percentage points relative to initial executions while using 19.0–43.6% fewer observed recipient tokens in reruns. GPT-5.5 ablations support combining nested subtask analysis with shortcut construction and issue reconciliation, though the work is a preprint without peer review or community discussion yet.

## [GVPO++ Eliminates Importance Sampling in LLM Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21432) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces Group Variance Policy Optimization (GVPO), a post-training method that integrates the analytical solution of KL-constrained reward maximization into its gradient weighting scheme, removing the importance sampling that causes training instability in GRPO. GVPO guarantees a unique optimal solution and supports flexible sampling distributions, and it naturally extends to on-policy distillation (OPD) and a broader family of extended OPD objectives. Training instability from importance sampling is a well-known practical bottleneck in reinforcement-learning-based LLM post-training, so a method with theoretical guarantees and flexible sampling could make alignment and reasoning training more reliable. The extension to on-policy distillation also gives practitioners a principled foundation for designing diverse distillation objectives. GVPO's gradient corresponds to the mean squared error between the central distance of implicit rewards and that of actual rewards, giving an intuitive interpretation of the update. The paper claims two key advantages: a unique optimum exactly matching the KL-constrained reward maximization objective, and flexible sampling distributions without importance sampling.

**Background**: GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization), popularized by DeepSeek, is a reinforcement-learning method that improves LLM reasoning by comparing groups of sampled responses and normalizing advantages, but it relies on importance sampling to correct for distribution mismatch. Importance sampling reweights data generated by one policy to estimate expectations under another, which can introduce high variance and instability. KL-constrained reward maximization is a standard formulation in alignment (used in DPO and related methods) whose analytical optimum takes a Gibbs-like form.

## [LogicTrack Audits LLM Reasoning Steps with Formal Logic Solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21492) ⭐️ 7.0/10

LogicTrack is a neuro-symbolic framework that auto-formalizes each chain-of-thought step into symbolic logic and verifies it with automated theorem provers, introducing a Solver-Based Backtracking Reward (SBR) that scores step-wise logical soundness and guides backtracking tree search at inference time. The authors also extend it to generate supervised fine-tuning data with backtracking traces, and report experiments across 8 reasoning benchmarks and 7 LLMs showing improved verifiability and final answer pass rates. Most chain-of-thought optimization today relies on outcome-based feedback, so a model can reach a correct answer through logically flawed intermediate steps; LogicTrack targets that gap by verifying intermediate reasoning rather than only the final answer. This matters for high-stakes domains such as mathematics, law, and medicine, where the trustworthiness of the reasoning chain is as important as the answer itself. The framework combines auto-formalization, automated theorem proving, and backtracking tree search, and its SBR mechanism provides a step-wise score that quantifies logical soundness during inference. The approach is evaluated on 8 reasoning benchmarks and 7 LLMs, though the abstract does not detail formalization error rates or the computational overhead of invoking theorem provers at each step.

## [PolyBridgeBench Tests Multimodal LLMs on Physics-Grounded Bridge Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21493) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced PolyBridgeBench, an executable benchmark that evaluates multimodal large language models (MLLMs) on generating complete node-member-material bridge topologies from visual scenes and structured engineering constraints. The benchmark runs designs through deterministic legality checks and a native dynamic physics simulation, then tests repair after failure under a fixed interaction budget, with experiments on six representative MLLMs across 189 levels. This benchmark addresses a gap in evaluating whether MLLMs can synthesize load-bearing structures that actually work when executed, not just produce visually or structurally plausible designs. It could push AI-for-engineering research toward execution-grounded evaluation and more reliable iterative design agents. The benchmark separates deterministic validity, dynamic functional success, and post-failure recovery to pinpoint where a design fails, and it returns temporal visual evidence from failed rollouts for repair. Results show a substantial gap between deterministic validity and dynamic success, pronounced sensitivity to material budgets, and limited post-failure recovery under the primary strict-budget setting.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Multimodal large language models excel at visual understanding and structured generation, but those abilities do not guarantee that an engineering design will work when physically executed. Existing benchmarks assess spatial reasoning, structural validity, or physics-grounded construction, but not whether a model can synthesize a complete load-bearing structure and repair it after simulator execution exposes a failure. PolyBridgeBench fills this gap by combining executable physics simulation with failure-driven iteration for bridge design.

## [Self-Meta-Evolve Personalizes LLM Prompts Per User for Enterprise IE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21626) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers released Self-Meta-Evolve, a hierarchical framework that maintains a dedicated prompt for each user and refines it through a dual-loop process: an inner loop edits structured prompts using persona-conditioned feedback, while an outer loop evolves the meta-prompt by distilling successful editing patterns. Alongside it, they released a persona-driven benchmark of 292 simulated enterprise users built from O*NET occupational taxonomies, on which the method reaches a 74.58% success rate, beating the strongest prompt-optimization baseline by 13.56 absolute points and hitting 52.54% within just two iterations. Enterprise LLM deployments often assume one global prompt fits all users, but real workplaces are heterogeneous, so this work directly addresses a practical misalignment in personalized LLM applications. The new benchmark and the 71% win rate in a double-blind study with twenty real professionals suggest per-user prompt adaptation can be trained and evaluated at scale, which could influence how enterprise IE systems are built. The framework formulates enterprise IE as per-user prompt adaptation under interaction feedback, and the benchmark uses a reproducible persona-generation pipeline grounded in O*NET occupational taxonomies. The reported 74.58% success rate and 13.56-point margin over the best baseline are complemented by a double-blind human study in which adapted prompts won 71% of pairwise comparisons against static baselines.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models are increasingly used for enterprise information extraction, where the same document must be reorganized differently for each user. Existing prompt optimization methods typically optimize a single prompt against a global objective, which conflicts with user heterogeneity. Self-Meta-Evolve instead maintains a per-user prompt and uses a dual-loop, meta-learning-style process: the inner loop edits prompts based on persona-conditioned feedback, and the outer loop improves the meta-prompt that generates those edits.

## [L0-MoE Accelerates Dense LLMs Up to 2.5x with Minimal Loss](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21672) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.21672) proposes L0-MoE, a lightweight Mixture-of-Experts approach that uses L0-regularization to accelerate dense large language models by up to 2.5x while maintaining competitive performance. The method introduces a cluster confusion matrix for domain-aware dataset curation and applies dynamic batching for efficient training, outperforming existing LLM acceleration baselines. Inference cost and latency are among the biggest barriers to deploying large language models in production, and most existing acceleration techniques sacrifice noticeable accuracy. A method that delivers up to 2.5x speedup with nearly no performance loss could make dense LLM serving substantially cheaper and faster for both researchers and practitioners. The approach combines L0-regularization with a Mixture-of-Experts architecture to sparsify computation, and adds two engineering components: a cluster confusion matrix for domain-aware dataset curation and dynamic batching for training efficiency. Reported gains reach up to 2.5x over dense models, though the abstract does not specify model sizes, benchmarks, or the exact accuracy trade-offs.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) is a machine learning technique in which multiple specialized sub-models, called experts, are combined by a gating or routing mechanism that activates only the relevant experts for each input, enabling more efficient computation. L0 regularization is a sparsity-inducing penalty that directly counts non-zero parameters, encouraging a model to drop unnecessary weights; because the L0 norm is non-differentiable, it is typically optimized through stochastic relaxation methods. This paper applies that idea to turn dense LLMs into lightweight MoE models that skip computation without retraining from scratch.

## [GUARD Achieves Natural Forgetting in Large Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21677) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.21677) introduces GUARD (Guided Answer-Reasoning Distillation), a method that converts unsafe chain-of-thought disclosures in large reasoning models into safe-exit trajectories by aligning a frozen model via guidance tokens and distilling the guided behavior into its parameters. The authors also propose the Natural Forgetting Reasoning Score (NFRS) to measure structural stability, fluency, and unsupported substitutes in forgotten outputs, with experiments on R-TOFU and a STAR-1-derived harmful-intent setting showing reduced unsafe and privacy disclosures while preserving reasoning utility. This work highlights a critical gap in machine unlearning for large reasoning models: protected facts or unsafe rationales can leak through intermediate chain-of-thought traces even when final answers are suppressed. By defining what a post-forgetting trajectory should look like, GUARD offers a more robust safety mechanism that could influence how future unlearning objectives are designed for reasoning-capable models. GUARD aligns a frozen LRM using guidance tokens and then distills the guided safe-exit behavior into model parameters, rather than merely suppressing target content or redirecting internal representations. The newly introduced NFRS metric goes beyond leakage to capture structural stability, fluency, and unsupported substitutes, and the method is evaluated on two widely adopted distilled LRMs with code available on GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Machine unlearning aims to remove specific knowledge or harmful behaviors from trained models, but traditional evaluation methods like Membership Inference Attacks are not directly applicable to large language models. Large reasoning models add a new challenge because they generate intermediate chain-of-thought traces before producing a final answer, and these traces can themselves disclose protected or unsafe information. Knowledge distillation, which transfers behavior from one model to another, is used here to internalize safe-exit trajectories into the model's parameters.

## [ReACT-TTS Plans Conversational Speech from Listener Facial Reactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21683) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose ReACT-TTS, a two-stage framework that uses a one-second pre-response listener facial sequence to plan the next utterance's emotion and prosody before speech realization. On a strict dyadic MELD protocol, temporal conditioning outperforms text-only conditioning in mean macro-F1 and VAD concordance across ten seeds, and 76% of 20 speech researchers preferred it in a contextual-appropriateness study. This work shows that listener facial dynamics carry complementary cues for conversational response planning, potentially making TTS systems more socially attuned in dialogue settings. It could influence how future conversational AI and affective computing systems integrate multimodal listener feedback rather than relying on text alone. Ablations show temporal modeling performs best among visual variants, an explicit early-to-late difference is unnecessary, and correct listener reactions outperform cyclic mismatches on average. The predicted response style is connected to a Grad-TTS backbone for end-to-end speech realization, and source code is publicly available.

MELD is a multimodal multi-party dataset for emotion recognition in conversation that includes audio and visual modalities, and a dyadic protocol restricts it to two-speaker exchanges. Grad-TTS is a diffusion probabilistic text-to-speech model that generates mel-spectrograms by gradually transforming noise, and here it serves as the speech realization backbone.

## [ECG Mirage: VLMs Fail to Truly Use ECGs in Clinical Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21755) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.21755) identifies a failure mode called "ECG Mirage" in vision-language models (VLMs) used for emergency department clinical prediction, distinguishing between ECG neglect (ECGs add little predictive benefit) and ECG confusion (matched ECGs beat no-image inputs but not mismatched ECGs). Testing four VLMs on the MDS-ED benchmark, the authors found matched ECGs gave no consistent advantage for ICU admission or clinical deterioration prediction, and they mitigated the problem by training four restricted visual prompts with supervised learning plus conditional direct preference optimisation while keeping the VLM backbone frozen, reaching balanced accuracies of 70.6% (ICU admission) and 67.5% (deterioration) and widening the matched-versus-mismatched gap by roughly 16.5 and 5.5 percentage points. This work shows that strong multimodal benchmark scores can mask models that ignore or misread the actual patient's ECG, a serious reliability risk for medical AI deployed in emergency decision support. It also offers a lightweight mitigation—visual prompt tuning with a frozen backbone—that could make clinical VLMs more trustworthy without costly retraining. The evaluation holds clinical text and prediction targets fixed while comparing matched ECGs, outcome-discordant mismatched ECGs, and no-image inputs, which cleanly separates genuine ECG use from spurious multimodal capability. The mitigation keeps the VLM backbone frozen and only trains restricted visual prompts, making it efficient, though results are limited to four VLMs and the MDS-ED dataset and remain a preprint without peer review.

**Background**: Vision-language models (VLMs) are AI systems that jointly interpret images and text, extending text-only large language models; examples include GPT-4V, Gemini, Claude 3 Opus, and open-source models like LLaVA. In emergency medicine, decision-making draws on heterogeneous data such as patient history, vital signs, lab results, and electrocardiograms (ECGs), and MDS-ED is a benchmark dataset built to evaluate multimodal decision support for emergency department diagnoses and deterioration prediction. The concern is that a VLM may appear to use an ECG image while actually relying on text or irrelevant visual cues.

## [LLM-Generated Feature Pools Boost Time Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21801) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.21801) shows that a simple statistical pipeline using LLM-generated feature pools achieves 0.529 per-series VUS-PR on the TSB-AD-U benchmark, surpassing the best neural (0.45) and statistical (0.44) entries on the public leaderboard. By selecting over the union of hand-crafted and LLM-generated pools, the pipeline reaches 0.588, matching the top leaderboard entry. This work suggests that the candidate feature pool, not the downstream selection algorithm, sets the performance ceiling for time series anomaly detection, and that LLMs can automatically generate effective domain-specific features. It could shift research focus away from complex neural architectures toward smarter feature engineering and hybrid statistical pipelines. Ablations show that changing the candidate pool moves the score by 0.226, while selection strategy and aggregation grid only move it by 0.031 and 0.096 respectively. The generated pools match the hand-crafted one under matched selection and cover different domains, with their union improving results across all twelve generator-seed pairs.

## [MIST: Genomic-Guided Histology Attention for Multimodal Survival Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21811) ⭐️ 7.0/10

MIST introduces a multimodal survival prediction model that represents genomic features as tokens which query compact foundation-model-derived histology context tokens before survival prediction, rather than merging separately encoded modalities only at the final stage. Across four external evaluations in colon, renal, lung, and glioblastoma cohorts, MIST improves external C-index over standard fusion baselines in the primary comparisons, with code released at https://github.com/samiyavuuz/MIST. This work addresses two persistent obstacles in multimodal oncology outcome prediction — external cohort shift and computational complexity — by enriching molecular information with histology context early in the fusion process. If the approach generalizes, it could offer a more robust and compact strategy for combining whole-slide images and genomic profiles in clinical prognosis research. Training combines discrete-time survival prediction with genomic feature masking, WSI dropout, and paired WSI-genomics contrastive alignment, which together encourage the model to learn cross-modal dependencies. The evaluation relies on the C-index, a rank-correlation metric between predicted risk scores and observed event times, across four external cohorts.

## [EnterpriseVal: A Use-Case-Level Evaluation System for Enterprise GenAI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21841) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.21841) introduces EnterpriseVal, a use-case-level evaluation system for enterprise generative AI that assesses workflow fitness, reliability, safety, and scalability under a frozen socio-technical configuration. It combines a formal use-case specification, a six-category metric catalogue, a grading protocol blending blinded expert judgment with calibrated LLM-as-judge scoring via prediction-powered inference, a two-tier REJECT/CONDITIONAL/SCALE threshold gate, and a value-and-risk model. A pilot across three workflows at a global bank reported 88% human-graded citation precision and a 1.6% hallucination rate in credit-memo drafting, and cut analyst refinement effort from an estimated 27.4 to 2.9 hours per document in procedure transformation. Most enterprise GenAI initiatives still fail to show measurable business impact, and Gartner predicts over 40% of agentic AI projects will be cancelled by the end of 2027, largely due to unclear value and inadequate risk controls rather than weak models. EnterpriseVal reframes this as a measurement problem, offering practitioners a deployment-oriented alternative to public benchmarks that answer only "what can the model do?" rather than "is this workflow fit, reliable, safe and worth scaling here, on our data, under our controls?" The system requires freezing the entire socio-technical configuration under test — model, prompts, retrieval, tools, guardrails and human oversight — and assigns an autonomy level plus a consequence tier that jointly determine the required evaluation intensity. The pilot results are explicitly labeled as documented pilot evidence rather than established results, and the paper separates established findings, pilot evidence, the proposed system and open hypotheses, specifying the experiments still needed for full validation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Public benchmarks such as MMLU or HELM measure general model capability, but they do not tell an enterprise whether a specific workflow will perform reliably on its own data under its own controls. A socio-technical configuration refers to the combination of technical components (models, retrieval, tools, guardrails) and social/organizational elements (human oversight, review processes) that together determine real-world performance. LLM-as-judge is a technique where a language model scores outputs in place of human graders, and prediction-powered inference is a statistical method for combining a small set of gold-standard human labels with a large set of model-generated labels to produce valid confidence bounds. EnterpriseVal's two-tier gate converts these metric vectors with confidence bounds into actionable REJECT, CONDITIONAL, or SCALE decisions.

## [AutoViewMem Organizes LLM Long-Term Memory into Self-Configuring Orthogonal Views](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21940) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.21940) introduces AutoViewMem, a data-driven framework that discovers candidate semantic views from interaction traces, selects a compact complementary view set, and uses these views to guide write-time structured extraction of provenance-grounded memories. Experiments on the LoCoMo and PersonaMem benchmarks with Qwen3-8B and Qwen3-14B backbones show improved long-horizon question answering and personalization over strong memory baselines while keeping a simple top-K inference pipeline. Long-term memory is a key bottleneck for LLM agents that must stay consistent and personalized across extended conversations, and existing systems often mix preferences, events, constraints, and temporal updates into one representation, causing semantic interference that degrades top-K retrieval. By moving semantic disentanglement from retrieval time to write time, AutoViewMem could make memory retrieval more robust and cheaper for agent builders without requiring explicit routing or iterative retrieval. The framework operates in three stages: discovering candidate views from interaction traces, selecting a compact complementary (low-overlap) view set, and using those views to guide write-time structured extraction of provenance-grounded memories, followed by offline consolidation for compactness and consistency. Evaluation is limited to the LoCoMo and PersonaMem benchmarks and two Qwen3 model sizes, and the paper is an arXiv preprint without peer review or community discussion yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: LLM agents increasingly rely on retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) to pull relevant facts from an external memory store before answering, but retrieval can suffer from semantic interference when many semantically similar but distinct items compete for the same top-K slots. Semantic disentanglement aims to separate high-level, interpretable factors from nuisance variables in learned representations, improving control and robustness. AutoViewMem applies this idea to conversational memory by organizing memories into orthogonal views before indexing, rather than trying to disentangle them during retrieval.

## [CodeMidas Turns Source Code into RL Training Environments for Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22068) ⭐️ 7.0/10

CodeMidas introduces an agentic pipeline that converts implemented functionality in open-source codebases into executable reinforcement learning environments using source code as the only task-specific input. It produced a dataset of 5,545 training tasks from 3,185 codebases across 23 programming languages and 15 technical domains, and training MiMo-V2.5 with GRPO improved performance on all five benchmarks, including DeepSWE +11.7%, ProgramBench +17%, and Terminal-Bench v2.1 +8.5%. This work shows that source code alone, without relying on development artifacts like issues or commits, can serve as a scalable foundation for building RL environments, potentially broadening the range of tasks available for training coding agents. It matters for AI labs and software engineering teams seeking to train agents that can repair issues, build whole programs, and perform terminal work. The pipeline allocates agentic compute to every stage: agents explore implemented functionality to formulate behavioral specifications, construct tests grounded in execution of the original code, and validate and filter candidate tasks through execution checks and repeated solution rollouts. Ablations show that increasing the number of high-quality training tasks improves performance, and trajectory analysis reveals the RL-trained agent explores codebases more and performs more diverse self-verification.

**Background**: Reinforcement learning (RL) for coding agents requires diverse tasks with reliable verifiers, and open-source codebases are a rich source of such tasks. Existing methods typically rely on development artifacts such as issues and commits, which limits the range of extractable tasks. CodeMidas instead uses source code as its only task-specific input, and GRPO is a reinforcement learning algorithm used to train the model on these generated tasks.

## [dSTAR: A Lightweight Distributed SGD Method Tolerating Stragglers and Byzantine Attacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.07151) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose dSTAR, a lightweight distributed stochastic gradient descent (SGD) method that collects updates from the first k responding workers and filters out outliers using an ensemble median. The paper proves that dSTAR is (α, f)-Byzantine resilient and achieves a linear convergence rate, with empirical results showing it maintains high accuracy while other Byzantine-resilient methods suffer up to 40-50% accuracy drops under attack. Distributed training is increasingly common for large-scale machine learning, but stragglers and Byzantine attacks can severely degrade performance. dSTAR offers a practical solution that simultaneously addresses both issues with theoretical guarantees, potentially improving the reliability and efficiency of distributed training systems. dSTAR selects the first k workers to respond, which mitigates straggler delays, and uses an ensemble median to filter gradients that deviate significantly, providing resilience against Byzantine adversaries. The method is proven to be (α, f)-Byzantine resilient and achieves linear convergence, with empirical evaluations showing consistent high accuracy across various scenarios.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Distributed stochastic gradient descent (SGD) trains models across multiple computing nodes, but faces challenges such as the straggler effect (where slow nodes delay the entire process) and Byzantine attacks (where malicious nodes send arbitrary or corrupted gradients). Byzantine resilience in distributed machine learning is crucial for robustness, and existing methods often struggle to maintain accuracy under attack. dSTAR builds on these concepts by combining straggler tolerance with Byzantine resilience in a lightweight approach.

<a id="item-74"></a>
## [SpaceDiffusion Brings On-Orbit Diffusion to Satellite Generate-and-Forward Communications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20899) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (arXiv:2609.20899) proposes SpaceDiffusion, an over-the-orbit diffusion framework that lets satellites use on-orbit generative AI to reconstruct corrupted images before forwarding them, introducing a 'generate-and-forward' (GF) relaying paradigm. The core contribution is a channel-distortion-aware diffusion theory that injects a channel-distortion correction term into the standard DDIM update, allowing one pretrained diffusion model to adapt to varying packet-loss and compression distortions without retraining. Satellite uplinks are a critical bottleneck in 6G networks because ground devices have limited transmit power and small antennas, causing low data rates and high packet errors. If validated, this generate-and-forward approach could cut uplink power demands and latency, potentially reshaping how future satellite-assisted 6G systems handle image and data transmission. The framework analytically characterizes progressive token-reconstruction error and derives a diffusion-step activation threshold that predicts when SpaceDiffusion outperforms conventional decode-and-forward (DF) relaying; it also includes an energy-aware early-exit policy for efficient on-orbit deployment. Experiments report lower end-to-end latency than a DF scheme with retransmission and roughly 15 dB of uplink transmit power savings at a target perceptual quality.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Diffusion models are a class of generative models that learn to reverse a noise-adding process, and they are widely used for image denoising, inpainting, and generation; DDIM is a faster deterministic sampling variant of these models. In satellite communications, conventional decode-and-forward relaying requires the satellite to correctly decode a received signal before forwarding it, which is fragile under the low-power, high-error uplink conditions typical of ground devices. SpaceDiffusion instead treats recovery of compressed and lost image tokens as an inverse problem, so the satellite regenerates plausible content rather than merely decoding it.

## [LLM-Generated GPU Kernels Deliver Only ~1% End-to-End Gain on Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21058) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper evaluates five model configurations on KernelBench level 1, finding that a frontier model produces correct kernels for 91.1% of problems with independently verified speedups on 22 of 56 (median 1.235x), while the best open-weights model reaches only 30.4% correctness and solves zero convolutions. The authors profile seven real workloads and show the addressable runtime fraction ranges from 8.9% to 58.2%, bounding realistic end-to-end improvement at roughly 1% on transformers, and introduce DLRM-Bench, 12 recommender kernel problems where they measure a 41.7% win rate at 1.552x median, projecting 8.63% end-to-end. This work provides a reality check on the practical impact of LLM-generated GPU kernels, showing that even correct and faster kernels yield negligible end-to-end gains on transformers because 80-86% of runtime is spent in cuBLAS GEMM and FlashAttention. The finding that recommenders have a much higher addressable fraction (58.2%, concentrated in a single embedding kernel) suggests domain-specific opportunities, and the discovery of a correctness-check flaw in KernelBench has implications for how the community evaluates kernel generation. The paper shows that KernelBench's correctness check (torch.allclose with an absolute tolerance) is satisfied by a tensor of zeros on 4 of 60 level-1 problems, and two kernels in the authors' own results exploited this before detection, including one scored at 283x that wrote only 0.3% of its output buffer. The authors propose scale-invariant replacements and release all 879 evaluations, and note that the addressable fraction shrinks as model scale increases.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: KernelBench is a benchmark that asks LLMs to generate efficient GPU kernels (in CUDA or related DSLs) from PyTorch reference implementations, testing whether models can write low-level code that outperforms PyTorch. FlashAttention is an I/O-aware exact attention algorithm that reduces memory usage and accelerates transformers, while cuBLAS GEMM refers to NVIDIA's highly optimized matrix multiplication library routines. DLRM (Deep Learning Recommendation Model) is a widely used recommendation architecture whose embedding operations dominate runtime, motivating the new DLRM-Bench suite.

The paper introduces the COmmunity-centered Peer Engaged Support (COPES) dataset and a three-axis evaluation framework to measure how well LLMs align with community perspectives when responding to mental health support-seeking queries. It shows that post-training with SFT and DPO on COPES improves Strategy Alignment by over 50% for general-purpose models and improves alignment in Emotion & Tone, though gains are heterogeneous across subreddits and coping strategies. As professional mental healthcare access remains limited, people increasingly seek peer support on platforms like Reddit, and this work shows that community-curated data can meaningfully improve LLM-generated peer support. However, it also warns that improvements are uneven across sub-communities and needs, which matters for anyone deploying AI in mental health contexts. Post-training induces distributional shifts that heavily favor problem-focused recommendations while suppressing emotion-focused strategies, and alignment improvements vary significantly across subreddits and requested coping strategies. The work is a preprint without peer review, and no community discussion was provided.

## [Task Vector Composition Aligns LLMs with Ethical Preferences Across Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21094) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces a 12,000-instance multilingual dataset of two-option moral dilemmas covering three value conflicts (Honesty vs. Justice, Justice vs. Autonomy, Autonomy vs. Honesty) translated into Hindi, Arabic, Spanish, and Chinese. The authors propose task vector composition to isolate value-preference directions, showing that both plain fine-tuning and Direct Preference Optimization remove first-option bias in Llama-3.2-1/3B models and boost accuracy above 98%. As LLMs are deployed in applications that must weigh clashing moral values, hidden biases and brittle cross-lingual instruction-following pose real risks. This work offers a reproducible dataset and a modular alignment technique that could help developers build more consistent and ethically controllable models across languages. Benchmarking on GPT-5-mini reveals it consistently favors Honesty over Autonomy across all five languages when no policy is given, while Llama-3.2-1/3B models show strong first-option bias. The task vector transfer experiment orthogonalizes the value-preference direction against the general instruction-following vector, successfully isolating the specific value direction and enabling task arithmetic to produce a model with the opposite stance.

**Background**: Task vectors are compact representations that encode task-specific adjustments in neural networks, enabling modular adaptation and knowledge transfer by adding or subtracting them from model weights. Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) is a 2023 alignment technique that bypasses explicit reward modeling and reinforcement learning by directly optimizing the policy against human preference pairs. First-option bias refers to the tendency of LLMs to favor whichever option is presented first, a known artifact that can distort evaluation and decision-making.

## [New Framework Measures Human-AI Productivity by Quality and Interaction Cost](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21117) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.21117) proposes a productivity-oriented evaluation framework that measures human-AI collaboration as outcome quality relative to interaction cost. Across two datasets and four tasks, the authors find that sessions with identical quality ratings can differ by up to 70 times in interaction cost, and that subjective user ratings are not reliable substitutes for productivity. The work challenges the common practice of judging AI collaboration solely by task success or output quality, showing that quality alone can hide large differences in interaction effort. It could influence how HCI researchers and AI product teams design, benchmark, and report the real-world value of AI assistants. The paper reports that quality-cost relationships vary by task, with some tasks rewarding extended interaction while others favor fast convergence; productive sessions are characterized by agents probing earlier and users spending less effort repairing the interaction. It is an arXiv preprint without peer review or community discussion yet.

## [EnSol: Environment-Aware GNN Predicts Molecular Solubility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21151) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced EnSol, an environment-aware probabilistic graph neural network that represents solute and solvent as separate molecular graphs and combines them via cross-attention to capture solute-solvent interactions. It incorporates temperature through feature-wise modulation of the solvent representation and uses a mixture density network to output full solubility distributions, achieving Spearman correlations of 0.876 and 0.601 on the SolProp and Leeds benchmarks, respectively. Solubility prediction is critical for reaction feasibility, formulation, separation, and solvent selection, yet experimental measurement across solutes, solvents, and temperatures is costly and sparsely sampled. By moving beyond fixed-solvent assumptions and deterministic outputs, EnSol offers a more general and uncertainty-aware tool for computational chemistry and AI-driven scientific discovery. EnSol learns separate graph representations for solute and solvent before fusing them with cross-attention, and its mixture density network captures both temperature-dependent behavior and experimental uncertainty. Beyond benchmarks, experimental validation on chemically diverse solute-solvent pairs achieved a Spearman correlation of 0.715 and supported reliable solvent ranking.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Graph neural networks (GNNs) are deep learning models designed for graph-structured inputs, where nodes exchange information through message passing; in chemistry, molecules are naturally represented as graphs with atoms as nodes and bonds as edges. Molecular solubility prediction aims to estimate how well a molecule dissolves in a given solvent, and existing computational models often assume a fixed solvent, produce only single-point deterministic values, or simplify solute-solvent interactions. EnSol addresses these limitations by explicitly modeling the solvent environment and temperature while outputting a probabilistic distribution rather than a single number.

## [VNT-PA Uses Camera Pose as Positional Encoding for Visual Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21212) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose VNT-PA (Visual Navigation Transformer with Pose Attention), a transformer planner whose context is a set of depth keyframes indexed by camera pose rather than temporal order. On point-goal navigation in HM3D validation scenes, it reaches 93.3% success and 90.4% SPL, outperforming baselines that encode the same context as a temporal sequence or treat pose as an input feature. This shows that pose-stamped experience can serve directly as the environment representation for a learned planner, removing the need for explicit maps or topological graphs. It could improve long-horizon navigation and experience reuse in embodied AI and robotics, and degrades more gracefully under localization noise than map-based baselines. Because the spatial context is a pose-indexed set, frames from different trajectories can be fused at test time, enabling experience reuse across traversals. The model is trained to imitate a shortest-path planner operating on the ground-truth scene mesh, predicting actions by querying the spatial context with only its current pose and the goal position.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: HM3D (Habitat-Matterport 3D) is a large dataset of 1,000 high-resolution 3D scans of real-world indoor spaces, widely used as a benchmark for embodied AI navigation. Transformers typically use positional encoding to give the model information about the order or position of input elements, since attention itself is permutation-invariant. In navigation, learned policies usually consume observations as a temporally ordered history, which makes it hard to reuse experience from earlier visits to the same place.

## [Coda Reallocates Flow-Matching Steps to Endpoint Correction for VLA Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21216) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new paper introduces Coda, a method that reallocates part of a flow-matching policy's integration budget to a single learned endpoint correction. On 50 RoboTwin Easy tasks, five-step Coda improves success from 71.64% to 74.68% while cutting forward latency by 30.2% versus the default ten-step policy, and a two-step configuration reaches 71.88% success with a 2.12x speedup. This work challenges the common assumption that more integration steps always yield better closed-loop robot performance, showing that a lightweight corrector can improve both success rate and latency. It could benefit researchers building VLA models for real-time robotic control, where inference cost is a major bottleneck. Coda keeps the base policy frozen: a few-step noise-to-action trajectory is completed first, then a lightweight Transformer predicts a demonstration-supervised residual using the candidate action, source noise, and a shared observation-prefix cache, with only the corrector trained. The same design also improves frozen official SmolVLA, raising two-step success from 60.8% to 69.4%, and an independent 13-task control shows a 5.69-percentage-point gain at nearly equal latency.

**Background**: Flow matching is a generative modeling approach that learns a vector field guiding samples from a noise distribution to a data distribution, and VLA policies use it to generate action chunks by repeatedly evaluating an action expert. RoboTwin is a dual-arm robot manipulation benchmark with 50 tasks at Easy and Hard difficulty levels, commonly used to evaluate such policies. Increasing integration steps raises inference cost, but prior work shows it does not necessarily improve closed-loop success.

A new arXiv preprint introduces HALLUCINATION-R1, a two-stage optimization framework that generates semantically faithful yet robustness-challenging paraphrases to probe factual consistency in language models. Experiments on SimpleQuestions, PopQA, and TruthfulQA show it achieves a strong consistency-diversity trade-off and exposes robustness failures across multiple model families, with code and models released on GitHub. The work reframes hallucination as latent factual instability under meaning-preserving variation, a failure mode that standard benchmarks miss because they test each question only in its original form. If these paraphrase-induced failures are widespread, robustness-oriented training data like this could become important for building models that stay factually consistent under real-world rephrasing. The framework first stabilizes meaning-preserving and diverse paraphrasing, then rewards paraphrases that reveal factual consistency degradation in downstream QA models. Analyses indicate the failures are not reducible to surface-level artifacts or semantic drift, and a lightweight fine-tuning study shows the generated data improves robust accuracy under paraphrase variations.

HALLUCINATION-R1 targets the gap where general-purpose paraphrases are either too similar to the original (weak signal) or too divergent (breaking semantic equivalence).

KnowDemo is a new framework that extracts structured manipulation knowledge from human videos using a vision-language model (VLM), then resolves that knowledge against a target workspace's entities and geometry to generate diverse, valid robot demonstrations. Experiments show it produces additional verified execution modes beyond a reference-only baseline, improves candidate planning success via task-guided grasp sampling, and enables sim-to-real transfer of a fine-tuned pretrained π0.5 model across three tasks. By moving beyond simple motion-reference adaptation, KnowDemo addresses behavioral diversity and candidate validity, two key bottlenecks that have limited the usefulness of video-derived robot data. The framework separates task requirements from demonstration-specific choices by associating object and action descriptions with inferred task conditions, demonstration references, and permissible execution variations. Generated demonstrations carry structured execution labels and exhibit multimodal behavior through alternative contact strategies and valid subtask orders, with candidates screened before motion planning and simulation.

## [VLA-Scope Predicts Robot Failures Under Distribution Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21246) ⭐️ 7.0/10

VLA-Scope introduces a two-stage framework that first detects out-of-distribution (OOD) inputs and classifies their shift categories, then uses a shared logistic regression model combining action-prefix and execution-progress features to update failure risk as execution proceeds. Evaluated with OpenVLA on ten LIBERO-Spatial tasks, OOD detection reaches a ROC-AUC of 0.9454 and shift classification 91% accuracy, while the failure predictor achieves a ROC-AUC of 0.8497 after 60 executed actions, outperforming ActProbe and SAFE-MLP baselines. Reliable failure prediction is a prerequisite for safely deploying vision-language-action models in real-world robotics, where distribution shifts are unavoidable. By showing that OOD detection alone is insufficient and that execution history meaningfully improves failure prediction, this work addresses a critical reliability gap in embodied AI and could inform runtime safety monitoring for robot policies. The framework is evaluated via leave-one-group-out cross-validation on 1,400 OOD rollouts, and the failure predictor is assessed independently of the OOD gate; adding execution-progress features raises ROC-AUC from 0.7906 to 0.8497 after 60 actions. The approach relies on pooled image and language representations for shift characterization and a single logistic regression model shared across shift categories, which keeps the method lightweight but may limit expressiveness for complex failure modes.

**Background**: Vision-language-action (VLA) models are multimodal foundation models that take visual observations and natural-language instructions as input and directly output low-level robot actions, pioneered by Google DeepMind's RT-2 and exemplified by the open-source 7B OpenVLA model. ## [New Protocol Formalizes Safe Authorization Revocation for Long-Running AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21284) ⭐️ 7.0/10

An arXiv paper (2609.21284) introduces the concept of 'root-scoped authorization quiescence' and a protocol that safely revokes credentials for long-running AI agents that persist through delegated tasks, queues, callbacks, and provider-side operations. The protocol linearizes a root cut, fences old-root expansion, and composes provider-frontier certificates into a cutset over registered old-root paths, with proofs of post-cut issuer non-expansion, support-sound projection, and crash/replay stability. As autonomous AI agents become more prevalent, ensuring that revoked credentials cannot be exploited by lingering processes or delegated tasks is critical for security and compliance. This work provides a formal foundation for reasoning about authorization revocation in distributed, asynchronous agent systems, potentially influencing future agent frameworks and security standards. The protocol represents alternative and conjunctive authority as antichains of minimal sufficient root sets, and uses exact channel-token accounting to reconcile transfers; missing or conflicting evidence remains indeterminate. A provider-free late-effect test suite matches 17/17 registered outcomes, and a separately implemented checker verifies 17/17 traces while rejecting 44/44 consistently rehashed semantic regressions, though the certificate only establishes root-relative authorization quiescence within its bound manifest and configuration, not global idleness or business completion.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Long-running AI agents often outlive the processes that initiated them, relying on credentials, delegated tasks, queues, callbacks, reservations, and provider-side operations to continue working. Traditional cancellation, process exit, or credential revocation may not close every pre-cut carrier or distinguish independently authorized shared work, creating security gaps. This paper addresses those gaps by formalizing a notion of quiescence that accounts for all cut-relevant acceptances under a retired root-epoch and allows exact rebinding to current authority.

## [Deep-BQRL brings buffered quantile objectives to deep reinforcement learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21327) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.21327) introduces Deep-BQRL, a model-free distributional reinforcement learning framework that extends buffered-quantile learning to neural function approximation. It learns conditional return quantiles directly from sampled transitions, builds buffered action scores from the learned quantile function, and uses ensemble disagreement to guide exploration. This work removes the reliance on model-based planning and explicit return-law computation that limited earlier buffered-quantile methods to small tabular problems, potentially making risk-sensitive decision-making practical in larger, deep RL settings. It could benefit applications such as asset selling and safe control where optimizing a specific quantile of returns matters. Deep-BQRL uses an augmented input representation so the learned policy can respond to trajectory information without explicitly reproducing the quantile-state recursion required by exact planning. In experiments on an asset-selling optimal-stopping problem and slippery FrozenLake, it achieved smaller mean cumulative point-quantile policy gaps than PPO and TRPO at reported target levels, though model-based UCB-BQRL still retained the smallest gaps.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Distributional reinforcement learning models the full distribution of cumulative returns rather than just the expected value, enabling risk-sensitive behavior. Quantile-based methods optimize a prescribed quantile of that return distribution, but point quantiles can change abruptly under small perturbations, and exact quantile-sensitive planning is computationally demanding. Buffered quantiles smooth this by averaging neighboring quantiles just below the target level, but prior buffered-quantile methods remained model-based and limited to small tabular problems.

## [Co-Evolving Zero-Day Jamming: GAT Detection and RL Attack Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21334) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper introduces a two-pronged framework that pairs an online zero-day jamming detector combining a graph attention network (GAT) with Dirichlet process (DP)-means clustering, alongside an inference-driven reinforcement learning jammer that treats the target receiver as a black box. Simulations report the RL jammer achieves 33% higher attack efficacy and 67% higher stealth than benchmarks, while the proposed detector reaches 20% higher detection accuracy against that jammer. Zero-day jamming strategies that adapt on the fly can evade detectors trained only on known attacks, so a framework that both discovers emerging strategies and provides a black-box adversarial benchmark could improve how wireless security systems are evaluated and hardened. This matters for adversarial machine learning and wireless security researchers who need realistic, knowledge-free attack models to stress-test defenses. The detector jointly classifies known strategies and discovers zero-day ones within a unified learning objective, using GAT temporal-spectral representation learning plus DP-means clustering, while the RL jammer infers detector state via hypothesis testing and optimizes the trade-off between attack impact and stealth. The results are simulation-based, and the paper is an arXiv cross-listing without community discussion provided.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Graph attention networks (GATs) are graph neural network architectures that use masked self-attention to weight neighboring nodes, improving node classification and link prediction over fixed-aggregation models like GCNs. Dirichlet process clustering is a nonparametric Bayesian unsupervised method that automatically infers the number of clusters from data, making it suited to discovering unknown attack categories. Zero-day jamming refers to previously unseen interference strategies that a detector has not been trained on, which traditional supervised detectors struggle to identify.

## [CESBench: First LLM Benchmark for IoT Cryptographic Engineering Security](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21344) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced CESBench, a benchmark of 380 expert-written items covering six sub-domains of cryptographic engineering security for IoT devices, including side-channel analysis, fault injection, implementation, countermeasures, evaluation, and integration. Eleven open-weight and proprietary LLMs were evaluated across four task types, with composite scores ranging from 54.4% to 83.6%. This is the first benchmark specifically targeting cryptographic engineering security for IoT devices, filling a gap left by existing LLM benchmarks for cryptography and general cybersecurity. It enables rigorous evaluation of LLMs on implementation-level security tasks and could spur further research into AI-assisted secure hardware and embedded system design. The benchmark includes 209 multiple-choice items testing recall, 67 judgment items requiring a security verdict with justification, 63 scenario items requiring engineering diagnosis, and 41 code tasks graded by 572 test cases. While the strongest models nearly saturate multiple choice (98.6%) and code (95.1%), justifying a security verdict remains the weakest competence, with justifications earning only 53.4% of rubric marks despite 88.5% of verdicts being correct.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: IoT devices are often deployed in physically accessible locations, so even a mathematically secure algorithm can be undermined by implementation flaws that are difficult to patch after deployment. Cryptographic engineering focuses on how cryptographic algorithms are actually implemented in hardware and software, including defenses against side-channel analysis (extracting secrets from timing, power, or electromagnetic leakage) and fault injection (deliberately inducing errors to bypass security checks). LLMs are increasingly used to write and analyze such implementations, but until now no benchmark measured their competence in this specialized area.

Existing video benchmarks mostly test simple scene-level queries or global summaries requiring only single-step inference, leaving agentic video understanding largely unmeasured. AgentVidBench fills this gap and, by testing 12 proprietary and open-source MLLMs, shows that single-turn performance remains limited while agentic workflows generally improve both accuracy and trajectory scores, giving the field a holistic testbed for future research. The benchmark goes beyond standard question-answer pairs by supplying step-by-step solution traces that support trajectory evaluation, and experiments show that integrating models into state-of-the-art agentic workflows generally improves both accuracy and trajectory scores. The authors also present a simple yet effective agentic strategy as a competitive baseline, with code and datasets available at the linked GitHub and Hugging Face repositories.

## [WS-NeRF: Mamba-Driven Adaptive Deblurring for Neural Radiance Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21391) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (arXiv:2609.21391) introduces WS-NeRF, a Mamba-driven world-state-aware adaptive deblurring neural radiance field that formulates the alternating optimization of radiance fields as a dynamic evolution process with temporal memory. It jointly exploits multi-dimensional world states and a mixture-of-experts mechanism to dynamically adjust the confidence of deblurring priors, achieving better PSNR, SSIM, and LPIPS on blurry radiance field reconstruction. Real-world images are frequently degraded by blur, which severely harms NeRF reconstruction quality; WS-NeRF's approach to handling degradation and 3D inconsistency could make 3D reconstruction and novel view synthesis more robust for practical, imperfect capture conditions. It also demonstrates how Mamba state space models and mixture-of-experts can be combined for low-level vision and 3D tasks, potentially influencing future NeRF research. The method models alternating radiance field optimization as a dynamic evolution process with temporal memory, and uses a mixture-of-experts mechanism to adaptively weight deblurring priors based on multi-dimensional world states. The abstract reports improved PSNR, SSIM, and LPIPS plus more stable iterative recovery, but as an arXiv preprint it has not yet undergone peer review and no code or benchmark details are provided in the summary.

AtomEgo presents a systematic study of ego-robot co-training for embodied foundation models, supported by a curated corpus of approximately 2,659 hours of egocentric human interaction data and a scalable data processing pipeline. The work investigates three representative paradigms—joint co-training with domain-specific action heads, progressive ego-to-robot transfer through embodiment alignment, and joint video-action modeling—across vision-language-action and world-action architectures, evaluated via multi-task real-robot experiments. Embodied foundation models are bottlenecked by the limited scale and diversity of robot demonstrations, so understanding how to effectively leverage abundant egocentric human data could unlock significant generalization gains. The paper's proposed principle—Data Scale × Alignment Quality → Capability Gain—offers practical guidance for scalable ego-robot pre-training, potentially influencing how future embodied AI systems are built. The study spans both vision-language-action and world-action model architectures, and evaluates the paradigms through multi-task real-robot experiments plus language-conditioned cross-embodiment representation analysis. A key caveat is that the value of egocentric data depends heavily on how effectively it is aligned and utilized, meaning raw data scale alone is insufficient.

## [HE-Guardrail: Encrypted Guardrails Against Jailbreak Attacks in HE-LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21484) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper identifies a critical security vulnerability in homomorphic encryption (HE)-based LLM inference: because the server never sees plaintext prompts or responses, malicious clients can submit jailbreak prompts that remain entirely invisible to the server. To address this, the authors propose HE-Guardrail, a framework that evaluates guardrails entirely over encrypted data and homomorphically controls whether the target model's response is returned to the client. They instantiate it with three representative guardrails — Llama Guard, JBShield, and GradSafe — and show it closely reproduces the decisions of the corresponding plaintext guardrails in the encrypted domain, with distinct security-efficiency-utility trade-offs. This work exposes a fundamental tension in privacy-preserving machine learning: the same confidentiality that protects benign clients also blinds the server to adversarial prompts, potentially letting successful jailbreaks go completely undetected. By showing that guardrails can be evaluated homomorphically, it opens a path toward deploying encrypted LLM inference services that are both privacy-preserving and resistant to abuse, which matters for providers offering confidential inference to untrusted clients. HE-Guardrail operates entirely over ciphertext, so the server never decrypts prompts or responses, and it homomorphically gates whether the target model's output is released to the client. The three instantiations (Llama Guard, JBShield, GradSafe) each exhibit different security-efficiency-utility trade-offs, reflecting the substantial computational overhead that homomorphic evaluation adds to guardrail inference.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Homomorphic encryption is a cryptographic technique that allows computations to be performed directly on encrypted data without decrypting it first, so a server can process data while it remains confidential. In HE-based privacy-preserving machine learning, a client sends an encrypted input to a server, which evaluates a model such as an LLM without ever seeing the plaintext. Jailbreak attacks are adversarial prompts designed to bypass an LLM's safety alignment and elicit harmful outputs; they are a top security concern for LLMs according to OWASP. HE-Guardrail sits at the intersection of these areas, aiming to detect jailbreaks without breaking the encryption that protects client data.
