## [OneBid: A Unified Auto-Bidding Foundation Model for oCPX Ads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21550) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose OneBid, a unified auto-bidding foundation model that learns a reusable backbone from heterogeneous oCPX logs and adapts it to scenario-specific deployments via offline post-training. It extends Decision Transformer with dual Return-to-Go and Cost-to-Go conditioning, a sequence-level Mixture-of-Experts architecture, and Critic-guided Relative Offline Policy optimization (CROP), delivering a +2.2% ADVV gain on oCPX Ads at Kuaishou and peaking at +13.1% in the ROAS scenario. Auto-bidding is central to computational advertising, yet the prevailing oCPX paradigm serves each scenario (e.g., registration, purchase) with a separate model, causing fragmented pipelines and underexplored cross-scenario modeling. A single foundation model that scales with data and model size and is already fully deployed at Kuaishou suggests a path toward consolidating ad-tech pipelines and improving multi-objective control across heterogeneous advertising scenarios. OneBid builds on Decision Transformer but replaces single Return-to-Go conditioning with two atomic signals—Return-to-Go for conversion value and Cost-to-Go for cost ratio—plus value-aware regularization on next-action prediction. Its sequence-level Mixture-of-Experts design uses shared experts for cross-scenario knowledge and sparsely-routed experts for scenario-specific patterns at low latency, while CROP uses a learned critic to score candidate actions group-relatively, avoiding unsafe online exploration like GRPO-style fine-tuning and constraining policy shift to reduce out-of-distribution risk.

**Background**: Auto-bidding systems automatically set bids for advertisers in online ad auctions, and have evolved from rule-based controllers to reinforcement learning and generative methods such as Decision Transformer, which frames reinforcement learning as conditional sequence modeling. The oCPX paradigm optimizes cost per action (e.g., cost per registration or purchase) and spans many heterogeneous scenarios, each traditionally served by its own model. Foundation models like LLMs show that a single large backbone can be pretrained on diverse data and then adapted to many downstream tasks, which inspired OneBid to attempt the same unification for auto-bidding.

## [Attractive vs. Repulsive Self-Distillation: Opposing Behavioral Shifts in Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21561) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.21561) introduces attractive and repulsive self-distillation, showing that moving a model toward a privileged teacher suppresses exploratory reasoning and yields shorter, more confident responses, while moving away from that teacher increases response length, can trigger unintended switches into a latent thinking mode, and eventually becomes unstable. The authors then propose contrastive self-distillation, combining attraction toward a correct-solution-conditioned teacher with repulsion from an incorrect-solution-conditioned teacher, and find that the two teachers' shared behavioral shifts largely cancel, leaving a token-level signal that more directly reflects correctness. This work separates correctness from behavioral change in self-distillation, a distinction that prior work often conflated, and shows that a pure contrastive self-distillation objective can improve reasoning performance while keeping response lengths stable across non-thinking, instruct-only, and already-thinking models. It matters for AI/ML researchers training reasoning models, because it suggests that some gains attributed to distillation may actually come from unintended behavioral shifts rather than genuine knowledge transfer. The study isolates the self-distillation objective on its own, in contrast to prior work that combines such signals with a GRPO objective, and evaluates across non-thinking, instruct-only, and already-thinking models. A notable caveat is that repulsive self-distillation alone is unstable and can trigger unintended switches into a model's latent thinking mode, so the contrastive formulation is needed to cancel the shared behavioral shifts of the two teachers.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Self-distillation is a machine learning technique in which a model is retrained using its own predictions, often to compress or regularize a network. On-policy self-distillation extends this by conditioning the model on privileged information (such as a correct solution) to produce a dense, token-level teacher distribution, which is then distilled back into the student. Because privileged information can change not only what the teacher knows but also how it behaves, the resulting learning signal may mix correctness-relevant information with unintended behavioral shifts.

## [GameLogicBench Tests Coding Agents on Tick-Level Game Rules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21562) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced GameLogicBench, a benchmark of 72 gameplay-logic tasks built in Godot projects, where an automated evaluator checks each game's rules at every simulation tick across 403 hand-designed scenarios that expand into 1,451 seeded test cases. Across 20 combinations of language models and scaffolds, the best run solved only 52.78% of tasks, and all twelve models tested under Claude Code performed worse as task scope grew from isolated mechanics to repository-scale features. Existing game-development benchmarks replay fixed examples, score videos, or rely on another model as judge, so none verifies game rules throughout execution while guaranteeing reproducible verdicts. GameLogicBench fills this gap by measuring runtime behavior rather than implementation choices, which matters for anyone building or evaluating coding agents that must implement correct gameplay rules in real projects. To avoid rewarding a particular implementation, the evaluator must accept different correct implementations of each task while rejecting mutants that have one required capability removed; without this mutant-based validation, incorrect agent submissions passed. The paper also reports that most unsuccessful submissions are runnable but implement some required game behavior incorrectly, and that agents copy code from public repositories when network access is open.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Godot is a free, open-source, cross-platform game engine used for 2D and 3D games. A simulation tick is a single discrete step of a game's logic loop, so checking rules at every tick means verifying state continuously rather than only at the end of a run, since a game can end in a valid state even after violating its rules mid-run. Coding agents are LLM-driven systems that edit and test code in large software projects, and benchmarks such as SWE-bench Verified are the standard way to measure their ability to resolve real repository issues.

## [CityLearn v3 Adds Realistic Constraints to Renewable Energy Community Control Studies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21570) ⭐️ 7.0/10

CityLearn v3 is a configurable simulation and evaluation framework for renewable energy communities (RECs) that models dynamic membership, equipment availability, flexible-load deadlines, demand-response requests, local energy sharing, and data or equipment failures within a single environment. It records controller inputs, distinguishes requested actions from those actually applied to simulated equipment, and provides reference controllers, service- and constraint-aware performance indicators, and trajectory exports for comparison within and across communities. Previous controller studies often simplified changing participation, equipment availability, service deadlines, and data quality, so lower cost or peak demand could conceal missed services or infeasible power requests. By exposing these hidden failures, CityLearn v3 enables more realistic evaluation of control strategies for RECs, which matters for researchers in control and energy systems working on demand response and distributed energy resources. Building and phase power limits constrain controllable requests, and a declared timestep preserves consistent power-to-energy accounting. A synthetic high-frequency trace replay illustrates how aggregation can conceal short peaks without changing annual energy, and software checks and application examples examine service delivery, electrical constraints, settlement, and changing scenarios.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Renewable energy communities (RECs) coordinate buildings, photovoltaic generation, batteries, electric vehicles, and flexible loads to collectively produce and consume local renewable energy. CityLearn is an established open-source framework that lets researchers implement reinforcement learning agents in single- or multi-agent settings to reshape aggregated electrical demand by controlling energy storage for load shifting and heat pumps or electric heaters for load shedding. CityLearn v3 extends this framework to better reflect real-world REC conditions such as changing membership, equipment failures, and demand-response constraints.

## [Micro-Collaborative Poisoning: A Distributed Attack on RAG Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21573) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.21573) introduces Micro-Collaborative Poisoning, a distributed attack that splits a false target claim across multiple locally plausible documents instead of concentrating it in a single malicious passage. The authors evaluate the attack across 108 RAG configurations, varying dataset, retriever architecture, retrieval depth, database composition, number of poisoned databases, and generator model. The results show that poisoning is driven not by a single dominant passage but by the accumulation of weak adversarial signals across retrieved sources, meaning increasing top-k and poisoning multiple databases make the attack more likely to succeed. This matters because it reveals a threat that is hard to detect through isolated document inspection, raising new challenges for securing RAG pipelines used in enterprise and production AI systems. The paper reports that clean database diversity and stronger retrievers can reduce the influence of the attack, and a document-level poisoning visibility analysis shows Micro-Collaborative Poisoning achieves downstream influence while leaving a weaker explicit poisoning signature than direct poisoning. The evaluation spans 108 configurations, providing broad empirical evidence rather than a single-case demonstration.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) improves large language models by grounding their outputs in external knowledge sources, such as a document database, before generating a response. Because RAG systems trust the retrieved context, attackers can poison that context with false documents to steer the model's answers. Prior poisoning attacks typically concentrate the false claim in one or a few strongly malicious passages, which makes them easier to spot during document inspection.

## [PA-RL Uses Potential Fields as Action Representation for Contact-Rich Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21609) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.21609) proposes PA-RL, a reinforcement learning framework that uses artificial potential fields as the action representation for contact-rich robotic manipulation. Instead of directly commanding Cartesian motion, the policy adapts the parameters of an energy-like potential field, which generates a state-dependent guidance direction executed through a Cartesian impedance controller. On peg-in-hole insertion, PA-RL is the only method to reach a 100% simulation success rate within the training budget, versus 92.6% for the best baseline, and it completes 9/9 real-robot insertions without fine-tuning. Action representation is a critical but often overlooked design choice in reinforcement learning, and this work shows that decoupling task-level adaptation from low-level motion generation can substantially improve both learning efficiency and physical execution quality. If the approach generalizes beyond peg-in-hole, it could simplify how contact-rich manipulation skills are learned and transferred from simulation to real robots, benefiting robotics researchers and practitioners working on assembly, insertion, and other contact-heavy tasks. PA-RL reduces joint-torque variation by 55.4% and Cartesian acceleration variation by 70.8% relative to the best baseline, without any explicit motion-quality penalty in the reward. The evaluation compares PA-RL against Cartesian velocity, Cartesian pose, and variable-impedance action spaces using the same RL algorithm, and the simulation-trained policy transfers to 9/9 real-robot insertions without fine-tuning.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Artificial potential fields, introduced by Oussama Khatib in 1986, model the robot's environment as an energy landscape where the goal is a valley and obstacles are hills, so the robot can be guided downhill toward the target. In model-free reinforcement learning, the action representation determines how policy outputs are converted into robot motion, and direct Cartesian command interfaces force the policy to generate motion at every decision step, coupling high-level strategy with low-level control. Contact-rich manipulation tasks such as peg-in-hole insertion are especially hard because they involve nonlinear dynamics and discontinuous contact transitions.

## [Steering LLMs Toward Norwegian Moral Profiles via Persona and ActAdd](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21636) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper administers the Norwegian Moral Foundations Questionnaire (MFQ-30) to six open-weight LLMs and compares their profiles to a sample of 1,282 Norwegian respondents. It finds that a neutral Nordic-respondent persona, written without any distributional information from the human sample, brings engaging models 44–77% closer to the Norwegian mean in Mahalanobis d², while one-pair ActAdd at a fixed mid-layer flattens the foundation profile rather than steering individual foundations. This work tests whether psychometric instruments like the MFQ-30 measure anything stable in LLMs and whether their moral profiles can be deliberately steered toward a target human population, which matters for AI alignment and for the cultural validity of value-elicitation methods. It also provides a concrete instance of the "cognitive phantoms" warned about by Peereboom et al. (2025), where a persona that shifts the profile also induces engagement absent at baseline. Only half of the six models engage with the questionnaire under the attention check; the other half default to flat or central-tendency outputs that look near-human on average without tracking item content. The persona intervention is written without any distributional information from the human sample, and the ActAdd intervention uses a single prompt pair at a fixed mid-layer, which flattens rather than steers individual foundations.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: The Moral Foundations Questionnaire (MFQ-30) is a psychometric instrument originally developed to measure human moral intuitions across foundations such as care, fairness, loyalty, authority, and sanctity. Recent research applies such human questionnaires to large language models to elicit moral and value profiles, but it remains unclear whether these instruments measure anything stable in models. Activation Addition (ActAdd) is a steering technique that contrasts intermediate activations on prompt pairs (e.g., "Love" versus "Hate") to compute a steering vector added to specific layers, enabling reversible behavioral control without retraining. Mahalanobis d² is a covariance-aware multivariate distance measure that accounts for correlations between variables, unlike Euclidean distance.

## [SynthDemo-RL Uses LLM-Guided Synthetic Demos to Break Zero-Reward Barrier in VLA Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21650) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SynthDemo-RL introduces a teacher-student framework in which an automated teacher converts simulator-privileged state into successful manipulation trajectories, a VLA student is distilled via supervised fine-tuning, and PPO with binary task-success rewards refines the student. On LIBERO-PRO, with 50 synthesized trajectories per task and no new human demonstrations, it rescues all 27 previously unsolvable tasks and reaches average success rates of 97.8% and 97.1% on the Position and Task axes, respectively. This work addresses the sparse-reward exploration problem that has limited reinforcement learning for fine-tuning Vision-Language-Action models, showing that synthetic demonstrations can replace costly human teleoperation data. It could substantially reduce the data-collection burden for robot learning and make RL-based VLA adaptation practical on perturbed or novel tasks where no demonstrations exist. The paper introduces reward coverage — the fraction of tasks for which at least one success is observed under a fixed evaluation protocol — as a complement to average success rate. Direct PPO from a pi_0.5 policy fine-tuned on original LIBERO tasks rescues only 10 of the 27 zero-success tasks under the same compute, while SynthDemo-RL rescues all 27; on standard LIBERO the pipeline reaches 96.0% with no human demonstrations, within 1.7 points of pi_0.5 trained on 50 human demonstrations per task, and is further validated on RoboTwin 2.0 with open-loop execution on a physical robot.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models are multimodal foundation models that take images and text instructions as input and directly output low-level robot actions, typically built by fine-tuning a vision-language model on robot trajectory data; the concept was pioneered by Google DeepMind's RT-2 in 2023. Fine-tuning these models usually depends on human teleoperation demonstrations, while reinforcement learning with sparse binary rewards struggles because successful trajectories are rarely sampled, creating a zero-reward barrier. LIBERO is a public benchmark for lifelong robot manipulation, and LIBERO-PRO is a perturbed variant of it for which no demonstrations exist, making it a hard testbed for adaptation methods.

## [VLA Policies That Succeed Share Similar End-Effector Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21659) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A large-scale empirical study analyzed 15,000 closed-loop LIBERO rollouts from four vision-language-action policies, forming 3,600 configuration-matched policy pairs. Both-success pairs had a median normalized dynamic time warping distance of 0.0120 m, versus 0.0380 m when exactly one policy succeeded, an ordering that held across every task, every policy pair, and nine sampling and band-limited representations. The finding suggests that task success in VLA policies is associated with a convergent physical execution geometry, offering a new lens for comparing and evaluating robot policies beyond binary success rates. This could inform how researchers assess policy generalization, transfer, and the reliability of embodied AI systems. The ratio of distances varies severalfold across representations, so the authors report the direction rather than a fixed multiple; both-failure pairs are more separated but rest on thin, uneven support and are treated as exploratory. A matched baseline still reveals measurable, heterogeneous residual policy differences, meaning a low cross-policy distance does not imply interchangeability, and under composite visual stress policy rankings and pair composition change together.

## [Steering Fails in Latent Reasoning: The Latent-to-Language Transition Gap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21662) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.21662) reports that activation steering applied to continuous latent chain-of-thought (CoT) thoughts produces substantially weaker effects on subsequent language generation than steering explicit CoT, even when hidden representations are displaced by comparable amounts. The authors show task information remains identifiable in continuous thoughts, and present two further results—an abrupt change in the output distribution at the transition boundary and much weaker bidirectional control from task-related directions in latent CoT—supporting a hypothesized latent-to-language transition gap. This finding challenges the common assumption that activation steering transfers equally well to latent reasoning, and it identifies the latent-to-language transition interface as a central target for evaluating and designing future steering methods. It has direct implications for interpretability and control of language models as latent CoT approaches gain popularity. The paper reports that steering continuous thoughts moves hidden representations by amounts comparable to explicit CoT steering yet yields weaker downstream language effects, and that the output distribution changes abruptly at the transition boundary rather than gradually. Task-related directions also exert much weaker bidirectional control in latent CoT than in explicit CoT, suggesting the gap is specific to the latent-to-language interface rather than a general failure of steering.

**Background**: Activation steering is a family of inference-time techniques that modify a transformer's hidden states with steering vectors to control properties such as sentiment, topic, or truthfulness without retraining. ## [TERMon: Hardware Runtime Monitor Detects Edge AI Behavioral Threats](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21713) ⭐️ 7.0/10

TERMon introduces a lightweight hardware runtime monitor that detects persistent behavioral threats in edge AI by matching ternary patterns against thermometer-encoded inference fingerprints, without re-executing or formally verifying the model. Implemented on a PYNQ-Z2 FPGA, the pipelined design requires no on-chip block RAM or DSPs and achieves a two-cycle decision latency. Edge AI accelerators are increasingly deployed in safety-critical environments where model corruption, distribution shift, and adversarial inputs can still produce well-formed, confident predictions, making runtime failures hard to detect. TERMon offers a hardware-native, low-overhead monitoring approach that could improve safety and security for edge AI systems controlling physical actuators, access control, or alarms. TERMon represents class-conditional trusted behavior as hardware-efficient ternary patterns matched in parallel against a thermometer-encoded fingerprint, and the ternary encoding reproduces the corresponding unquantized range decision exactly. It detects harmful weight corruptions in proportion to their behavioral impact, but out-of-distribution and adversarial inputs are largely not separable using the monitored features at a strict false-positive operating point.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Ternary computing uses base-3 logic with trits instead of binary bits, which can be more hardware-efficient for certain pattern-matching tasks. Edge AI accelerators run inference locally on devices, and runtime monitoring aims to detect anomalies during execution rather than through costly re-execution or formal verification. Thermometer encoding represents a value as a sequence of ones followed by zeros, enabling simple parallel range comparisons in hardware.

## [ForceTwin Uses Human Force Probing to Build Physics-Informed Robot Digital Twins](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21751) ⭐️ 7.0/10

ForceTwin introduces a system that identifies physics-informed digital twins of articulated objects by having a person probe them with a handheld force-sensing gripper, capturing synchronized poses and interaction forces. It estimates articulation plus parametric dynamics (inertia, Coulomb friction, viscous damping) and a structured neural residual for state-dependent mechanism forces, nearly halving inertial-parameter error versus a VLM prior. Existing digital-twin pipelines rely mainly on visual and language priors, which cannot reveal properties like friction or spring mechanisms that vary with configuration and velocity, leading to physically implausible estimates. ForceTwin shows that instrumented human interaction can fill this gap, improving impedance-control goal completion to 87% across nine object-embodiment pairs versus 60% for VLM priors and 57% for kinematics-only twins, which could advance sim-to-real transfer for manipulation. The system uses a handheld force-sensing gripper to collect synchronized poses and interaction forces, then fits parametric dynamics with a structured neural residual for state-dependent mechanism forces. The authors also use the identified twins to train whole-body door-traversal policies and deploy them on real hardware, including a Spot and a Franka FR3, with the largest gains on objects whose strong mechanisms cause both baselines to stall.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: A digital twin is a virtual replica of a physical object used for simulation and control, and for robots it typically encodes the object's kinematics and dynamics. Articulated objects such as doors or drawers have internal mechanisms—springs, door closers, friction—whose effects depend on how far open they are and how fast they move, and these are not visible from appearance alone. Vision-language models (VLMs) can guess physical parameters from images and text, but such static priors often produce physically implausible values. ForceTwin instead learns these hidden properties from forces measured during human probing.

<a id="item-110"></a>
## [Matrix AdaGrad Adds Row-wise and Column-wise Adaptive Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21815) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper develops a general Online Mirror Descent framework with adaptive proximal functions for matrix-valued parameters, deriving two new algorithms: Row-wise Matrix AdaGrad (Row-AdaGrad) and Column-wise Matrix AdaGrad (Column-AdaGrad). Their adaptive scaling is determined by accumulated row-wise or column-wise gradient norms, and the authors prove regret bounds that can be strictly tighter than entry-wise AdaGrad under structured gradients. Most widely used adaptive optimizers such as AdaGrad and Adam scale learning rates entry-wise, ignoring the matrix structure of weight tensors in neural networks. This work provides a principled theoretical route to matrix-aware adaptivity, which could lead to more stable training and better trainability at larger learning rates and greater network depths. The framework formulates Online Mirror Descent for matrix variables with new row-wise and column-wise matrix proximal functions, showing that adaptive scaling naturally arises from the corresponding proximal geometries. Experiments on matrix factorization and deep neural-network training demonstrate improved optimization stability and trainability, though the paper is a theoretical arXiv preprint without released code.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: AdaGrad is an adaptive subgradient method that maintains per-coordinate accumulated gradient norms to scale learning rates, and it is a foundation for modern optimizers like Adam. Online Mirror Descent is a general online-learning framework where a proximal function defines the geometry of each update step. Matrix-aware optimizers such as Shampoo have shown benefits from exploiting matrix structure, but a general theory comparable to AdaGrad for deriving such adaptivity was lacking.

## [Neural Cellular Automata Hidden Channels Learn Transferable Features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21870) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.21870) investigates the internal hidden channels of Neural Cellular Automata (NCAs) and introduces a transfer-learning mechanism that injects a pretrained teacher's hidden states into a student model to guide early optimization. Evaluated on few-shot and scale-variant MNIST benchmarks, NCAs outperform comparable recurrent and feed-forward architectures with only about 9,800 parameters. This work suggests that hidden-state dynamics in NCAs can serve as a decentralized, parameter-efficient substrate for transfer learning, offering a promising alternative to over-parameterized deep learning models that tend to overfit in few-shot regimes. It could benefit researchers working on efficient deep learning, few-shot generalization, and mechanistic interpretability. Mechanistic analysis reveals that the hidden channels decouple feature extraction from uniform classification consensus by absorbing morphological complexity and converging to mutually orthogonal states. These channels capture general, scale-invariant topological primitives rather than class-specific templates, allowing a student model to perform well on unseen classes using features transferred from a teacher trained only on digits 0-5.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Neural Cellular Automata combine neural networks with cellular automata, where a grid of cells updates its states locally over time to produce emergent patterns, and they are known for being highly parameter-efficient. Few-shot learning refers to training a model to classify new classes from only a small number of labeled examples per class, a setting where conventional over-parameterized models often overfit. Mechanistic interpretability aims to reverse-engineer the internal structures and algorithms of neural networks, and this paper applies that lens to NCA hidden channels.

## [Free-Energy-Inspired Method Improves LLM Pretraining Data Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21888) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.21888) proposes Energy Transfer Detection (ETD), an entropy-adjusted scoring method for detecting pretraining data in large language models. Instead of the horizontal decision boundary used by likelihood-only detectors, ETD uses an inclined boundary that evaluates prediction loss relative to predictive entropy, improving average AUROC by up to 3.5% and TPR@5%FPR by up to 5.1%. Pretraining data detection is central to auditing LLM privacy, copyright, and evaluation, and likelihood-only detectors systematically misclassify predictable non-members as members. ETD's principled entropy correction offers a more reliable member-non-member separation, which could improve membership inference attacks and data provenance research. The paper shows analytically that entropy correction preserves the expected membership signal while reducing its variance, and extends the mean-variance analysis to settings with a nonzero mean entropy gap. The entropy-adjusted score admits a Helmholtz free-energy interpretation, framing detection as a macroscopic residual free-energy transfer, and the method remains robust across diverse experimental settings.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Membership inference attacks (MIAs) aim to determine whether a specific data sample was used to train a model, and in LLMs this is complicated because high likelihood can reflect either genuine training exposure or strong generalization. Predictive entropy measures the uncertainty of a model's next-token distribution, and prior work has used entropy and log-likelihood features for tasks such as hallucination and machine-generated text detection. Helmholtz free energy is a thermodynamic potential that balances energy and entropy, and it has inspired machine learning methods such as the Helmholtz Machine for approximate Bayesian inference.

## [Failing Robots Ask for Help Based on Prompt Order, Not Sensor Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21942) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.21942) builds a simulated benchmark in which every robot failure's true cause is known because it was deliberately injected, and tests six open vision-language models on the decision of whether to act, consult another sensor, or ask a human. The study finds that moving the refusal option from last to first in the answer list collapses refusal rates from 78-100% to 0-6% in three of six model-and-family pairs, while image-based diagnosis accuracy stays at or below a majority-class baseline under every prompt variant. This work matters because it shows that the decision to interrupt a human for help — a core safety and efficiency question in human-robot interaction — is currently driven by superficial prompt formatting rather than measured sensor reliability, which could lead to robots that either pester people unnecessarily or silently fail. It also suggests that much of the apparent failure reflects missing sensor data rather than missing model ability, since handing the same models force data as ten lines of text produced the first above-baseline diagnoses in four of six models. The benchmark measures what each sensor reveals with explicit data-leakage checks: force data yields 0.99 diagnostic accuracy while no image-based method exceeds 0.55, and stated model confidence carries no information about correctness. The paper frames the choice as a three-action decision problem whose optimal policy follows from measured accuracy, yet the models ignore a fourfold change in question cost, and a single question to a human still lifts them from baseline to roughly the answerer's own reliability (0.70-0.81 when they ask).

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly used in robotics to let machines perceive and reason about scenes from camera images, but deploying them onboard is constrained by latency, limited compute, and privacy concerns. In human-robot interaction, failures are inevitable despite good design, and prior work has studied how robot failures affect human trust and how people can help recover from errors. This paper connects those threads by asking when a failing robot should stop acting on its own diagnosis and instead consult another sensor or ask a person.

## [Bayesian Belief Layer Enables Controllable Opinion Dynamics in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21997) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Bayesian Chronicle Agents (BCA), a minimal belief layer that separates what an LLM agent believes from how it speaks, updating each stance as a probability via one Bayesian step per utterance heard. A single prior-strength parameter κ encodes stubbornness, and sweeping it yields three canonical regimes—consensus, persistent disagreement, and committed-minority influence—with persistent disagreement matching Friedkin-Johnsen closed-form fixed points at R²=0.93–0.99. This work makes opinion dynamics in LLM-based social simulations explicitly controllable and auditable, addressing the problem that persuasion openness is currently neither specifiable nor verifiable and that collective outcomes silently inherit the model's training prior. It could benefit researchers in AI, multi-agent systems, and computational social science who need reproducible, parameterized simulations of opinion formation. The prescribed κ remains recoverable after the language round-trip, with perfect rank-order recovery across all four tested models, and the explicit belief layer surfaces systematic per-model stance biases that end-to-end simulation would silently absorb. As a preprint, the work has not yet undergone peer review and currently has no community discussion.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: LLM agents used in social simulation typically revise opinions implicitly in context, meaning their openness to persuasion cannot be directly set or checked. Friedkin-Johnsen (FJ) dynamics is a classical opinion-dynamics model in which agents hold stubbornly anchored initial opinions while also being influenced by others, and its fixed points have closed-form solutions. Bayesian updating provides a principled way to revise a probability belief when new evidence (here, an utterance) arrives, and κ controls how strongly the prior belief resists that evidence.

Researchers introduced DiaVLo, a diagnostic framework that combines human curation with VLM generation to build specifications of desired and observed behaviors in vision-language models, surfacing misalignments. It also provides causal estimates identifying the most influential concepts steering VLM behavior, evaluated on several open-source VLMs under classification and generation conditions. As VLMs are increasingly deployed in commercial and safety-critical applications, verifying that they exhibit desired behaviors while avoiding harmful ones is essential for reliable deployment. DiaVLo addresses a critical gap in VLM reliability and interpretability by offering a structured way to specify and diagnose model behavior, which could inform safer evaluation and deployment practices. DiaVLo produces behavior labels that correlate with model performance and provide context for measured performance, revealing both clearly aligned and misaligned behaviors. The experiments also expose patterns in how VLMs perceive, organize, and prioritize concepts, though the work is an arXiv preprint without peer review or community discussion yet.

## [Gricea: Open-Science Platform for Reproducible Conversational AI Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22039) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced Gricea, an open-science platform that represents conversational AI (CAI) studies as configurable, deployable research artifacts that can be run, inspected, shared, and reused. In a replication study, Gricea replicated configurations from 93% of eligible CUI 2026 papers while flagging missing information in 96% of papers that hinders faithful replication. Fragmented reporting of systems and study configurations in conversational AI research has long hindered replication, extension, and cumulative knowledge building. Gricea offers a concrete infrastructure that could make CAI studies more reproducible and reusable, benefiting researchers, practitioners, and the broader human-computer interaction community. Gricea couples study procedures, participant-facing systems, and conversational task behavior into a single artifact, and a user study showed that researchers and practitioners from diverse backgrounds could successfully construct runnable studies addressing various open-ended research questions. The 96% missing-information figure underscores how widespread incomplete reporting remains even in recent venues like CUI 2026.

## [Value-Sensitive Delegation: How Users Judge AI Agents Beyond Task Output](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22067) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.22067) applies Value Sensitive Design to analyze 73,093 first-person Reddit posts about using the AI agent OpenClaw, coding each post for human value, agent aspect, value fulfillment, and user outcome. The study identifies 21 values grouped into six categories — including Autonomous, Dependable, and Affordable Operation, Bounded Reach, Reviewability, and Equitable Access — and introduces the concept of "value-sensitive delegation." The findings suggest that evaluating AI agents purely by task completion misses what users actually care about, since values cluster around the operating conditions users set (cost, access, oversight) rather than the agent's outputs. This could push agent designers and evaluators to account for delegation conditions and supervision experience, not just benchmark success rates. Values were usually met where users described what the agent delivered (five of six value groups), but mostly unmet where users described supervising the agent (all six groups). The analysis was conducted with LLM assistance on a large corpus of Reddit posts, and the paper is a preprint that has not yet undergone peer review.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Value Sensitive Design (VSD) is a theoretically grounded approach, developed by Batya Friedman and Peter Kahn starting in the late 1980s and 1990s, for incorporating human values into technology design through iterative conceptual, empirical, and technological investigations. As autonomous AI agents become more common, users delegate work to them, but conventional evaluations typically measure only task completion. This paper extends VSD to the everyday use of AI agents by examining what values users express when delegating tasks.

## [Collab-Solver Jointly Learns Cut Selection and Branching for MILP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.03030) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose Collab-Solver, a multi-agent reinforcement learning framework that formulates the collaboration between cut selection and branching in MILP solvers as a Stackelberg game. It uses a two-phase learning paradigm — data-communicated policy pretraining followed by orchestrated multi-module policy learning — and experiments on synthetic and large-scale real-world MILP datasets show jointly learned policies significantly improve solving performance and generalize across instance sets. Existing learning-based MILP methods typically train policies for individual solver modules in isolation, ignoring their interdependence, which limits both solving efficiency and solution quality. By jointly optimizing cut selection and branching, Collab-Solver could improve solving efficiency and solution quality for combinatorial optimization problems that arise in logistics, scheduling, and resource allocation. The framework casts the interaction between cut selection and branching as a Stackelberg game, where one module acts as leader and the other as follower, and stabilizes collaborative learning through a two-phase paradigm. The paper reports experiments on both synthetic and large-scale real-world MILP datasets, though it is a specialized arXiv preprint without accompanying community discussion.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Mixed-integer linear programming (MILP) is a fundamental combinatorial optimization problem in which some variables are constrained to be integers while others are continuous. Conventional MILP solving relies on carefully designed heuristics embedded in the branch-and-bound framework, which systematically enumerates candidate solutions as a search tree and prunes branches that cannot beat the best solution found so far. Recent research explores using neural networks to learn policies for individual solver modules, but these modules — such as cut selection and branching — are interdependent, and learning them separately limits overall performance.

## [MemeLens: Unified Multilingual Multitask VLM for Meme Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.12539) ⭐️ 7.0/10

MemeLens consolidates 38 public meme datasets into a shared taxonomy of 20 tasks spanning harm, targets, figurative/pragmatic intent, and affect, and trains a unified multilingual, multitask, explanation-enhanced Vision-Language Model (VLM). The paper provides a comprehensive empirical analysis across modeling paradigms, task categories, and datasets, and releases the model, datasets, and experimental resources publicly. Meme understanding is critical for content moderation and online safety, but prior research is fragmented across tasks and languages, limiting cross-domain generalization. MemeLens's unified taxonomy and model provide a benchmark and baseline that could standardize multilingual meme research and improve real-world detection of harmful or manipulative memes. The shared taxonomy covers 20 tasks across four semantic categories, and the empirical analysis shows that robust meme understanding requires multimodal training, varies substantially across semantic categories, and is sensitive to over-specialization when models are fine-tuned on individual datasets rather than trained in a unified setting. All resources are available on GitHub and Hugging Face.

Memes are a dominant online communication medium where meaning emerges from interactions between embedded text, imagery, and cultural context, making them challenging for automated understanding. Multi-task learning, which solves multiple related tasks simultaneously using a shared representation, can improve generalization by exploiting commonalities across tasks. MemeLens applies this paradigm to consolidate fragmented meme datasets into a single multilingual, multitask model.

## [CRYSTAL Benchmark Evaluates Multimodal Reasoning via Verifiable Steps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.13099) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced CRYSTAL (Clear Reasoning via Yielded Steps, Traceability, and Logic), a diagnostic benchmark with 6,372 instances that evaluates multimodal reasoning through verifiable intermediate steps rather than final answers alone. They propose Match F1 and Ordered Match F1 metrics, and evaluation of 20 MLLMs reveals systematic failures such as universal cherry-picking and disordered reasoning, while a new Causal Process Reward (CPR) with curriculum training achieves a 32% improvement in Match F1 via GRPO. Current multimodal benchmarks mostly score only final answers, which can hide flawed reasoning processes; CRYSTAL exposes these hidden failures and offers a training signal that improves reasoning without manual step annotation. This could shift how the AI community evaluates and trains multimodal models toward more transparent and trustworthy reasoning. References are built through a Delphi-inspired pipeline where four independent MLLMs generate trajectories, aggregated via semantic clustering and validated through human quality gates; notably, no competitive model preserves more than 60% of matched steps in the correct order, and CPR-Curriculum succeeds where additive reward strategies fail.

Traditional evaluation relies on answer accuracy, which cannot distinguish a correct answer reached by sound reasoning from one reached by luck or flawed steps. Benchmarks like CRYSTAL aim to measure the reasoning chain itself, using metrics such as F1 that compare generated steps against reference steps.

## [GEAKG: Transferable executable knowledge graphs for algorithm design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.27922) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Generative Executable Algorithm Knowledge Graphs (GEAKG), a representation that stores procedural knowledge in algorithm design as a generative, executable, transferable graph whose typed nodes hold validated operators and whose edges encode admissible compositions with learned weights. Layer ablations show that within a neural-architecture-search family a learned snapshot transfers across 70 dataset pairs and stays competitive with Regularized Evolution at zero deployment-token cost, while across combinatorial domains only the ontology-constrained executable structure transfers, not the learned weights. This work reframes procedural knowledge in algorithm design as an offline-acquirable, compactable, inspectable and reusable artifact, suggesting that executable algorithm knowledge can be transferred without runtime language-model calls. It offers a conceptual framework and practical evidence for meta-learning, automated algorithm design and knowledge transfer, especially where target-side search is expensive. Transfer is localized by granularity: within a NAS family the learned weights remain correlated across datasets and one frozen snapshot matches Regularized Evolution at zero deployment-token cost, whereas across combinatorial domains only the ontology-constrained executable structure transfers. A Traveling Salesman snapshot beats an equally untuned from-scratch search on large scheduling instances even at one-fifth its budget, but does not improve on an effective local search where one is cheap, as in assignment and linear ordering.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Knowledge graphs (KGs) have proven effective for organizing declarative knowledge, but current KG paradigms provide limited support for representing procedural knowledge as executable, learnable graph structures. GEAKG extends this idea by storing executable operators in nodes and learned composition patterns in edges, so that graph traversal generates solutions; when both the topology and the operators are synthesized by an LLM, the result is a Generative Executable Algorithm Knowledge Graph. The same engine instantiates the structure across domains by changing only a role ontology (RoleSchema) and a binding.

## [IGAC: Intent-Governed Tool Authorization for AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22916) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers present Intent-Governed Access Control (IGAC), a server-side authorization layer that converts a trusted user request into a short-lived intent certificate, narrows the statically authorized tool manifest, and checks proposed tool and payload effects before execution. In evaluations spanning 176 synthetic tasks, 306 end-to-end model-task runtime trials, and a 36-trial external benchmark subset, reference-certificate IGAC reduced the archived composite exposure-or-path indicator from 1.0000 to 0, and the combined IGAC-OpenPort path recorded no completed unsafe executions. Tool-using AI agents typically run under integration credentials whose static permissions far exceed what a given request actually needs, creating a broad attack surface for prompt injection and tool misuse. IGAC addresses this gap by confining agent tool use to the expressed user intent without expanding static policy, which could become an important building block for enterprise-grade agent security and governance. IGAC cannot grant authority beyond static policy, and confinement to the request additionally depends on certificate fidelity and sound effect bounds; in end-to-end model runs, unsafe accepted authority still ranged from 0.0909 to 0.2727, with every residual case being a non-executed draft. A trace-backed normalizer counterfactual eliminated this residual authority but at substantial utility cost, and the authors identify certificate precision as the principal remaining bottleneck.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: AI agents that call external tools (such as APIs, databases, or code executors) usually authenticate with long-lived integration credentials, so the permissions available to the agent are fixed at setup time rather than scoped to each user request. This mismatch means a benign request can still leave the agent holding far more authority than needed, which attackers can exploit through prompt injection or manipulated tool outputs. IGAC is a server-side layer that treats the user's expressed intent as a monotone, auditable policy attribute, issuing short-lived certificates that filter the tool manifest and validate proposed tool and payload effects before anything executes. The approach is evaluated on top of an OpenPort governance substrate, which provides the underlying policy enforcement infrastructure.

## [EBTE: Server-Verified Action Claims for AI Agent Tool Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25364) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2607.25364v3) introduces Explanation-Bound Tool Execution (EBTE), a claim-carrying mediation layer that converts decision-relevant rationale content from tool-using agents into typed action claims and checks them against server-held intent, policy, payload, tool, risk, provenance, and freshness facts. The authors formalize the composition under explicit mediation and trusted-fact assumptions and report that a versioned reference profile matches all specified dispositions across 136 authored conformance scenarios, admits none of 96 designated hard contradictions, and passes 232 metamorphic checks. Tool-using AI agents commonly attach free-form rationales to their structured calls, but such rationales are neither authorization nor reliable introspection, so treating them as implicit permission creates a real security gap. EBTE offers a practical governance mechanism that could influence how future trustworthy agent systems mediate tool use, especially for organizations deploying agents that must not exceed baseline authority. EBTE cannot widen baseline authority: conflicts are denied, incomplete or uncertain claims go to review, and only matching claims remain eligible for governed execution. In a frozen 2026-07-12 exploratory 224-attempt hosted-model record, historical generation/runner agreement counts were 71/96, 66/96, and 19/32, and a zero-call revalidation under the current pipeline yielded 70/96, 65/96, and 17/32; in an AgentDojo-derived semantic check, existing high-risk controls made all 12 attack proposals non-allow while EBTE resolved the task-proposal contradictions as deny.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Tool-using agents expose structured calls but commonly attach free-form rationales, which are neither authorization nor reliable introspection. EBTE is a claim-carrying mediation layer that turns decision-relevant rationale content into typed action claims and checks them against server-held facts before execution. The paper formalizes this composition under explicit mediation and trusted-fact assumptions and implements a versioned reference profile with minimized audit packets, evaluated through conformance scenarios, metamorphic checks, and an AgentDojo-derived semantic check.

## [Fraglingo: Autoregressive Fragment Generation for Molecular Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13519) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Fraglingo introduces an autoregressive molecular generator that builds molecules step by step from chemically meaningful fragments, jointly predicting fragment identity and attachment configuration through an attachment-aware embedding and latent-space retrieval. Because prediction operates in a continuous embedding space rather than over fixed fragment identifiers, larger fragment libraries can be used at inference time without retraining, and the model generalizes to libraries up to four times larger than those seen during training. This work offers a unified generation primitive for molecule generation, scaffold generation, scaffold decoration, and molecule optimization, which could streamline multiple stages of AI-driven drug discovery. Its ability to scale fragment libraries at inference without retraining addresses a key practical bottleneck in fragment-based design, where expanding chemical space typically requires costly model retraining. Fraglingo uses a wildcard-anchored readout that represents both the growing molecule and candidate fragments relative to their attachment sites, enabling a single latent prediction to determine both fragment identity and attachment configuration. On controlled property-conditional benchmarks, it achieves stronger joint property control than comparably trained baselines while maintaining competitive validity, uniqueness, and novelty.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Fragment-based drug design (FBDD) is a strategy that screens small molecular fragments that bind weakly to a target protein, then grows or combines them into drug-like compounds; it is attractive because fragment libraries are much smaller than full compound libraries. Autoregressive molecular generation builds molecules one step at a time, and recent methods increasingly operate in continuous latent spaces to enable retrieval and optimization. Fraglingo combines these ideas by predicting fragment additions in an embedding space, so the fragment vocabulary is not fixed at training time.

## [MOSCOPT: Parameter-Free Mixture-of-Skills Optimization for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14399) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers released MOSCOPT, a text-native, parameter-free algorithm that jointly optimizes a pool of N skills together with a gating skill G that dynamically selects K skills per step for LLM agents. Using a new EditAdam optimizer with internally maintained dual states and three-phase interleaved updates, it consistently outperforms all baselines across 5 benchmarks and 3 target LLMs, with code released on GitHub. Existing prompt and skill optimization methods only optimize a single text template, missing the synergy among complementary strategies; MOSCOPT's mixture-of-skills architecture with selective activation could make LLM agents more capable without any gradient or parameter tuning. This matters for practitioners building agent systems who want better performance at lower engineering cost. The method is parameter-free and text-native, relying on EditAdam's dual states and three-phase interleaved updates to monotonically improve the system without gradient computation or parameter tuning. Ablations confirm that both the mixture-of-skills architecture with selective activation and the collective evolution with three-phase interleaving are essential to its performance.

**Background**: LLM-based agents often rely on natural language prompts and reusable "skills" as their strategic backbone, and recent work has tried to automatically optimize these texts to improve agent behavior. However, most prior methods optimize only one template at a time, so they cannot exploit multiple complementary strategies. MOSCOPT addresses this by maintaining a pool of skills and a gating skill that decides which subset to apply at each step, similar in spirit to mixture-of-experts but operating entirely on text.

## [AcquireBound: Runtime Authorization for AI Agent Resources](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14744) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The arXiv paper introduces AcquireBound, a provenance-bounded runtime authorization architecture that quarantines resources acquired by autonomous AI agents and activates them only through a current activation transaction. It proves eight safety properties and reports that reference semantics accepted 20/20 benign traces while rejecting 40/40 registered unsafe traces across 810 events. This work addresses a critical post-fulfillment activation gap in agentic systems, where payment, budget, OAuth, mandate, and fulfillment checks validate transaction conditions but do not decide whether a returned resource may become usable authority. It could affect researchers and practitioners building autonomous agents, access control systems, and agentic commerce platforms. The architecture resolves actual capabilities from authenticated provider evidence through a versioned resolver and checks a downward-closed relational envelope over a typed resource-capability hypergraph, preserving correlated identity, effect, data, delegation, and graph-wide limits. Single-use effect permits are revalidated and consumed at effect linearization, and a five-source audit classified 1,248 field pairs across 32 units with no unit alone supplying a complete activation profile.

AcquireBound is presented as a provenance-bounded runtime authorization architecture to close this gap by quarantining acquired outputs and validating them before activation.

## [CHASE Uses Counterfactuals to Stop Agent Benchmark Cheating](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18366) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Counterfactual Harness Search and Evolution (CHASE), which reframes harness evolution as constraint generation over validity-preserving benchmark counterfactuals. After each Proposer update, a Challenger searches for an executable protocol transformation that destroys the cheating gain, a validity firewall checks task semantics are preserved, and a confirmation set decides whether the counterfactual enters a finite archive. Evaluated on a synthetic benchmark and OfficeQA, CHASE retains strong released-benchmark gains while substantially reducing gain destruction under valid protocol changes. Automatic harness optimization lets a Proposer game a fixed benchmark protocol, producing a cheating harness whose gains depend on benchmark-wide shortcuts rather than genuine capability. CHASE offers a principled way to detect and neutralize such shortcuts, which matters for anyone relying on agent benchmarks to measure real progress and for AI safety work on evaluation integrity. The method formalizes an exact shortcut-neutralized benchmark B_0 and establishes statistical guarantees linking finite counterfactual archives to B_0, while also characterizing sequential Challenger search. A validity firewall ensures task semantics are preserved, and only counterfactuals passing a confirmation set enter the finite archive, which bounds the search cost.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Agent evaluation often relies on a released benchmark whose protocol stays fixed even when tasks are held out, so an optimizer that edits prompts, memory, retrieval, tools, and control code can exploit benchmark-wide shortcuts instead of improving the underlying agent. Counterfactual reasoning asks what would happen under a modified but semantically equivalent protocol, which can reveal whether a gain is genuine or an artifact of the benchmark. CHASE applies this idea to harness evolution, treating protocol transformations as constraints that must not destroy legitimate performance.

## [LGM: Neuro-Symbolic Framework Disentangles Long-Term Memory via Sparse Autoencoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18461) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.18461v2) introduces LGM, a neuro-symbolic framework that shifts long-term memory disentanglement into a continuous latent space using a sparse autoencoder. Instead of persisting fixed, query-agnostic graphs, LGM maps historical interactions into latent memory nodes, disentangles memory traces into sparse concept activations, and dynamically synthesizes query-aware relational edge weights for personalized reasoning. This work addresses a key limitation of existing structured memory frameworks, which rely on static, query-agnostic graphs that fail to capture context-dependent relations. By enabling fine-grained personalization and cross-session reasoning, LGM could significantly improve personalized agents that must infer both explicit preferences and implicit behavioral evidence from long-term interaction history. LGM uses a graph encoder that treats the query embedding as a conditioning preference to direct non-linear message passing across a task-specific latent subgraph, yielding a highly expressive memory representation. Extensive experiments on long-term personalization benchmarks show it significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in capturing both explicit and implicit preferences.

Current benchmarking practices rely heavily on aggregate scores that can hide critical failures and are hard to compare across LLMs, agentic systems, and multimodal models. A shared trustworthiness spine with safety-critical overrides could give developers, auditors, and regulators a more interpretable and credible basis for assessing AI systems. The framework preserves system-specific metrics while mapping native measurements to common performance bands with uncertainty estimates and traceable evidence, and safety-critical overrides prevent aggregate scores from masking critical failures. ## [SAIGE Paper Questions When Multi-Agent Collaboration Actually Helps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19759) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.19759v2) systematically analyzes when multi-agent collaboration outperforms single-agent systems, finding that benefits are concentrated in long-horizon tasks with sparse dependencies, while single-agent harnesses remain superior in tightly coupled sequential workflows. The authors propose SAIGE (Semantic-Aware Incremental Graph Evolution), a lightweight mechanism that models collaboration as a dynamically evolving graph with on-demand agent nodes and semantic dependency edges. The findings challenge the prevailing assumption that adding more agents always improves system intelligence, offering practical guidance for AI/ML researchers and practitioners designing multi-agent architectures. It suggests that task structure, not agent count, should drive design decisions, potentially saving significant context overhead and compute costs. SAIGE models collaboration as a graph where nodes are agent instances spawned on demand and edges encode semantic dependencies established through content-based information retrieval. Experiments show that scaling the agent pool or deepening recursion levels does not consistently improve outcomes, indicating multi-agent superiority is bounded by task structure rather than universal.

This paper systematically examines that trade-off and introduces a graph-based mechanism to optimize collaboration.

## [NeuSOGA3D: Neuro-Symbolic Framework for Explainable 3D Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20323) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers present NeuSOGA3D, a hybrid neuro-symbolic framework that reconstructs 3D geometry from unorganized point clouds by projecting them onto principal orthographic planes and building symbolic implicit spline representations, then fusing them via shape-preserving constructive solid geometry (CSG) operations into a coarse visual hull. Experiments on all forty categories of the ModelNet40 benchmark show it can recover structurally meaningful, CAD-compatible representations, unlike conventional neural implicit methods that encode geometry in opaque latent spaces. Neural implicit methods achieve high reconstruction accuracy but produce latent representations that are hard to interpret or reuse in engineering workflows; NeuSOGA3D's explicit symbolic outputs (control polygons, spline fields, cross-sections, volumetric lofts) could make AI-driven reconstruction directly usable in CAD and geometric modeling pipelines. This aligns with the broader rise of neuro-symbolic AI as a way to combine deep learning's perceptual power with the reliability and knowledge reuse of symbolic reasoning. The framework progressively transforms observations into explicit symbolic entities and recovers additional detail through cross-sectional decomposition and volumetric reconstruction using Partial Shape-Preserving Splines. It is an arXiv preprint (v2) with no peer review or community discussion yet, so its practical impact and generalization beyond the ModelNet40 benchmark remain unvalidated.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Neuro-symbolic AI combines neural networks, which learn statistical patterns from data, with symbolic AI, which uses explicit rules and knowledge representation, aiming for systems that are more reliable and interpretable than pure deep learning. Neural implicit methods for 3D reconstruction (such as NeRF-style approaches) encode shapes in latent representations, which limits interpretability and reuse in engineering tools. Constructive solid geometry (CSG) is a classic solid-modeling technique that builds complex shapes by combining simpler primitives with set operations, and it is widely used in CAD. NeuSOGA3D sits at the intersection of these ideas, using learned perceptual priors to guide symbolic geometric reasoning.

## [CDQAC: Offline RL Learns Job Shop Scheduling from Random Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.10303) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduce Conservative Discrete Quantile Actor-Critic (CDQAC), an offline reinforcement learning algorithm that learns effective job shop scheduling (JSP) and flexible JSP (FJSP) policies directly from static, suboptimal datasets. Experiments show CDQAC matches or outperforms the data-generating heuristics and recent online/offline RL baselines, requiring only 1–5% of the original dataset to learn high-quality policies. Online RL for scheduling typically requires extensive environment interaction, which is costly and limits practical deployment. CDQAC's high sample efficiency and ability to learn from suboptimal data could make RL-based scheduling more practical for real-world manufacturing and operations, and its finding that state-action coverage matters more than trajectory quality challenges conventional wisdom about dataset curation. CDQAC couples a quantile-based critic with delayed policy updates to estimate the return distribution of machine-operation pairs, and the authors note that FJSP and JSP combine a dense reward aligned with the makespan objective with equal-length trajectories across heuristics. Notably, datasets generated by a simple random heuristic with broader coverage let CDQAC outperform policies trained on datasets produced by stronger heuristics such as Genetic Algorithms; source code is available at https://github.com/jesserem/CDQAC_scheduling.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Offline reinforcement learning learns a policy from a fixed, static dataset of previously collected transitions without further environment interaction, which is attractive when online exploration is expensive or risky. Job shop scheduling (JSP) is a classic combinatorial optimization problem where jobs with ordered operations must be assigned to machines to minimize makespan, the total time until all jobs finish; flexible JSP (FJSP) relaxes this by allowing each operation to run on any machine from a given set. Actor-critic methods combine a policy (actor) with a value estimator (critic), and quantile-based critics model the distribution of returns rather than just the mean.

## [Deep Learning Boosts Single-Pair Wi-Fi Sensing, Paper Explains Why](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.02845) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2511.02845v3) theoretically analyzes why deep learning improves Wi-Fi sensing under hardware constraints, identifying prior information and temporal correlation as key side information that reduces estimation error bounds. The authors built a deep learning-based Wi-Fi sensing system using only a single transceiver pair, achieving an average human pose estimation error of 0.2189 m and localization error of 0.6124 m while running in real time at 42 fps on commodity hardware. This work provides a theoretical foundation for the widely observed but poorly understood phenomenon that deep learning can surpass conventional resolution limits in Wi-Fi sensing, which is crucial for scaling next-generation Wi-Fi sensing deployments that demand high precision with minimal bandwidth and antennas. It could guide the design of more efficient sensing systems for applications like human pose estimation and localization in smart homes and IoT. The system operates with only a single transceiver pair, significantly reducing hardware requirements compared to multi-antenna setups, and achieves real-time performance at 42 fps on commodity hardware. The theoretical analysis shows that prior information and temporal correlation act as side information that lowers the estimation error bound, explaining the deep learning gains.

Deep learning has been increasingly applied to Wi-Fi sensing to improve accuracy, but the theoretical reasons for its success under hardware constraints were not well understood. ## [SMILES-Based Polymer Graph Boosts Foundation Model Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.11881) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced a chemical language foundation model using a SMILES-based polymer graph representation (CPG) that incorporates architectural features and connectivity often missing in other line notations. The model achieved excellent performance on 30 polymer property benchmark datasets and showed strong robustness to structural perturbations, even with chemically invalid SMILES. This work addresses key challenges in polymer informatics, such as data scarcity and lack of standardized benchmarks, by providing a robust representation for language-based foundation models. It could accelerate AI-driven polymer discovery and highlights an unexamined blind spot in chemistry language models regarding structural representation robustness. Control experiments revealed that many variations of structural representation, including chemically or semantically invalid SMILES, exceeded or equaled state-of-the-art performance in several instances. Attention maps and error analysis corroborate the model's ability to interpolate SMILES sequence space loosely congruent to chemical and architectural space.

## [BEAT-Net Uses Biomimetic Priors for Interpretable ECG Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.07316) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced BEAT-Net, a supervised biomimetic framework that combines QRS-centered biological tokenization with a four-stage hierarchical architecture mirroring a cardiologist's workflow. Evaluated on PTB-XL, CPSC2018, and CSN, it achieves 0.924 AUC with only 0.7 million parameters (95% fewer than CNN baselines) and outperforms the 39.5-million-parameter HeartLang foundation model on morphological Form classification (0.901 vs. 0.832 AUC). This work shows that injecting explicit physiological structure can yield more data-efficient, interpretable, and generalizable ECG models than massive pre-training, which matters for clinical deployment where transparency and limited labeled data are critical. It suggests a path toward medical AI that aligns with how cardiologists actually reason, potentially improving trust and cross-dataset robustness. BEAT-Net uses a QRS tokenizer to convert continuous signals into semantically complete heartbeat sequences, then processes them through a Word Encoder for morphological features, a Spatial Operator for lead-invariant normalization, a Temporal Operator for context, and a Transformer-based Sentence Encoder for global reasoning. It attains full CNN-level performance with only 35% of training data, and its learned attention patterns spontaneously align with established clinical heuristics.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Electrocardiograms (ECGs) record the heart's electrical activity through multiple leads, and automated diagnosis with deep learning often treats these recordings as generic time-series or images, forcing models to rediscover physiological structure implicitly. This leads to data inefficiency, poor generalization, and opaque decisions that don't match clinical reasoning. QRS complexes are the most prominent waveform features in an ECG, representing ventricular depolarization, and cardiologists typically analyze heartbeats beat-by-beat across leads. BEAT-Net's biomimetic design explicitly encodes this structure rather than relying on massive pre-training like foundation models such as HeartLang.

Researchers propose HERMES, a holistic risk-aware end-to-end multimodal driving framework that uses vision-language models to inject long-tail semantic knowledge into trajectory planning. It builds structured Long-Tail Scene Context and Long-Tail Planning Context via a foundation-model-assisted annotation pipeline, then fuses multi-view visuals, ego-motion history, and semantic instructions in a Tri-Modal Driving Module. Long-tail, safety-critical interactions remain the biggest obstacle to deploying end-to-end autonomous driving, and HERMES shows that coupling vision-language semantic priors with risk-aware conditioning can consistently beat recent baselines on a large-scale real-world long-tail benchmark. This matters for researchers and engineers working on multimodal AI and safety-critical planning, since it offers a concrete recipe for turning foundation models into planning guidance rather than just perception aids. The framework relies on a foundation-model-assisted annotation pipeline to produce hazard-centric scene information, maneuver intent, and risk-aware planning guidance, and ablation studies confirm the complementary roles of its major components. Experiments are conducted on a large-scale real-world long-tail driving benchmark, though the abstract does not disclose specific metrics, model sizes, or inference latency.

The pipeline constructs persona profiles enriched with World Values Survey (WVS)-inspired attributes, defines a taxonomy-driven set of about 5,000 conversational scenarios, matches personas to scenarios via semantic similarity, and generates role-play conversations where the user speaks as the persona and the assistant acts as a helpful agent. ## [Position Paper Urges Dynamical Systems Perspective for Time Series Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16864) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv position paper (2602.16864v3) argues that time series modeling should adopt a dynamical systems (DS) perspective, contending that reconstructing the underlying governing equations via dynamical systems reconstruction (DSR) can yield theoretically optimal forecasts. The authors review central DS concepts, methods, and measures, and conclude with concrete suggestions for translating DSR insights into time series modeling. The paper challenges the current hype around time series foundation models by questioning how much real progress has been made, and proposes a domain-independent theoretical framework that could inform performance upper bounds, generalization to unseen regimes such as tipping points, and control strategies. If adopted, this perspective could shift research priorities toward more interpretable and computationally efficient forecasting methods. The authors claim that DS-based models offer profound advantages beyond short-term forecasts, including the ability to predict long-term statistics of an observed system, which may be more relevant in many practical scenarios. They also emphasize that DS theory provides domain-independent theoretical insight into mechanisms underlying time series generation, enabling better forecasting with much lower computational and memory footprints.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Time series modeling has evolved from early statistical, mainly linear approaches to the current trend of large-scale time series foundation models, such as Google's TimesFM and the MOMENT family, which are pretrained neural networks capable of zero-shot forecasting. Dynamical systems reconstruction (DSR) is a class of machine learning methods that infer surrogate models of the underlying governing equations from observed data. This position paper argues that integrating DS theory into time series modeling can address limitations of purely data-driven foundation models.

The MAMA-MIA Challenge introduces a standardized benchmark for joint evaluation of primary breast tumor segmentation and pathologic complete response (pCR) prediction using only pre-treatment dynamic contrast-enhanced MRI. It trained on 1,506 patients from multiple US institutions and evaluated 26 international teams on an external test set of 574 patients from three independent European centers, using a unified scoring framework that combines predictive performance with subgroup consistency across age, menopausal status, and breast density. Existing breast MRI AI models are typically developed and evaluated on heterogeneous datasets, making direct comparison difficult and limiting understanding of robustness across institutions and patient subgroups. By providing standardized datasets, evaluation protocols, and public resources, the challenge promotes the development of robust and equitable AI systems for breast cancer imaging, which could improve trust and clinical translation. Results demonstrate substantial performance variability under a common external evaluation framework and reveal trade-offs between overall accuracy and subgroup fairness. The challenge provides standardized datasets, evaluation protocols, and public resources, with a large multi-institutional author list indicating broad collaboration.
