## [HEAR Framework Brings Real-Time Sound-Centric Manipulation to Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.16086) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2603.16086v2) formalizes the Vision-Sound-Language-Action (VSLA) paradigm and introduces HEAR, a framework that integrates streaming audio with vision, language, and proprioception for continuous robotic manipulation. HEAR combines a streaming Historizer, an Envisioner adapted from omni foundation models, an Advancer audio world model, and a flow-matching Realizer policy, alongside the new OpenX-Sound pretraining dataset and HEAR-Bench benchmark. Existing Vision-Language-Action models treat sound as a static pre-execution prompt or focus only on human speech, leaving robots blind to fleeting environmental acoustics that could verify task state during execution. By addressing this gap, HEAR points toward multi-sensory foundation models for embodied agents that can react to dynamic real-world sounds in real time. The paper identifies a Blind Execution Interval caused by action chunking with open-loop execution, where acoustic events are lost between discrete audio observation windows, and argues that robust sound-centric manipulation requires causal persistence and explicit temporal learning. It also releases OpenX-Sound for pretraining and HEAR-Bench, described as the first sound-centric manipulation benchmark with strict causal timing rules, with code and videos at https://hear.irmv.top.

**Background**: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models map camera images and natural-language instructions directly to robot actions, and have become a major approach for general-purpose robotic manipulation. Many such systems use action chunking, predicting and executing a sequence of actions in open loop, which improves stability but means the robot does not react to new sensory input until the chunk finishes. Audio has recently been added to some VLA models, but typically only as a static prompt or for speech recognition rather than as a continuous control signal.

## [Robot Skill Updates as Software Lifecycle: Negative Results on Reproducibility and Governance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.07799) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2604.07799v3) treats robot skill updates as a software-lifecycle problem with a fixed planner dispatching versioned skill modules and a runtime shield screening actions. On six robosuite tasks it reports three negative results: peak task success is unstable across random seeds (23.3% to 73.3%), the four documented phase modules are actually whole-task policies rotated on a clock, and the shield cut violations 98–100% but left success at zero because its acceptance criterion omitted completions. This work challenges common assumptions in robot learning evaluation and deployment: single-run peak success is shown to be unreliable for ranking methods, and documented modular decompositions may not reflect actual runtime behavior. It also demonstrates that naive safety shields can trivially achieve perfect violation metrics by halting the robot, a critical lesson for long-lived autonomous systems where updates must be governed without destroying utility. The paper reports an exact p=0.029 for the retention difference between a whole-task policy (0.734) and the clock rotation (0.023) at four seeds per arm, and shows that restoring the termination condition the clock replaced raises retention on every seed. The promotion gate kept all twelve injected regressions out at a 22.5% clean-candidate rejection cost, while the dip detector caught nine of twelve regressions but missed all three on one seed.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Robosuite is a widely used simulation benchmark for robot manipulation, built on the MuJoCo physics engine, offering standardized tasks for reproducible evaluation. In long-lived robot systems, skill modules are updated over time, raising software-engineering concerns such as versioning, rollback, and runtime governance. A runtime shield is a safety layer that filters or rejects actions deemed unsafe before execution, while a promotion gate is a release mechanism that blocks regressions from being deployed.

## [Benchmark Shows Fused Code-Value Tokens Improve Medical Event Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.16775) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper benchmarks tokenization decisions for generative medical event models, training 156 models on full hospitalizations using Llama and Qwen architectures across three initialization seeds. The study finds that fused tokens pairing medical codes with value deciles improve predictive performance across all eight outcome families, with AUROC gains of +0.002 to +0.033 and Spearman correlation gains of +0.025 to +0.114. Tokenization is a foundational but underexplored design choice in medical AI, and this large-scale empirical study provides practical guidance for practitioners building generative models of patient timelines. The findings could influence how healthcare organizations structure electronic health record data for downstream classification and regression tasks, potentially improving clinical prediction accuracy. Neither anchoring value bins to reference ranges nor increasing quantization granularity consistently improved performance, and xVal variants underperformed both discrete and soft encodings. Alternatives to explicit time tokens, such as event-order and admission-relative rotary position embeddings (RoPE), yielded higher family-mean point estimates across all eight families while reducing input length, and mapping to the Common Longitudinal Intensive Care Unit Data Format (CLIF) reduced token count to 28.6% of native sequences while improving performance in six of eight outcome families.

Tokenization is the process of converting raw data into discrete units that a model can process, and in healthcare, this involves encoding heterogeneous data like diagnosis codes, numeric lab results, and temporal information. This paper systematically evaluates different tokenization strategies to determine which choices lead to better learned representations of patient data.

## [REALM aligns event streams with RGB foundation models via LoRA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.00271) ⭐️ 7.0/10

REALM introduces a cross-modal framework that learns an RGB- and Event-Aligned Latent Manifold by projecting event representations into the pretrained latent space of RGB foundation models using low-rank adaptation (LoRA). It enables zero-shot application of frozen image-trained decoders such as MASt3R to raw event data and achieves state-of-the-art wide-baseline feature matching. This work bridges the gap between event-based vision and powerful RGB foundation models, potentially unlocking generalizable event-based perception for robotics and multimodal tasks like depth estimation and semantic segmentation. It could reduce the need for task-specific event architectures and accelerate adoption of event cameras in challenging real-world scenarios. REALM leverages LoRA to bridge the modality gap without task-specific training, effectively unlocking geometric and semantic priors of frozen RGB backbones for asynchronous event streams. Downstream tasks are performed by simply transferring linear heads trained on the RGB teacher, and the method maps events into a ViT-based foundation latent space.

**Background**: Event cameras are neuromorphic sensors that asynchronously report per-pixel brightness changes, offering high temporal resolution, low latency, and robustness to extreme lighting compared to standard frame-based cameras. Vision Transformers (ViTs) are transformer-based foundation models for computer vision, and LoRA is a lightweight adaptation technique that adds low-rank matrices to a pretrained model to customize it without full retraining. REALM combines these ideas to align event data with the rich priors of RGB foundation models.

A new arXiv paper (2605.06445v2) systematically studies how LLM coding agents handle structural constraints in multi-file backend generation, introducing the concept of 'constraint decay.' Across 80 greenfield tasks and 20 feature-implementation tasks spanning eight web frameworks, evaluated configurations lost an average of 27.28 points in assertion pass rates from baseline to fully specified tasks. This work highlights that jointly satisfying functional and structural requirements remains a key open challenge for coding agents, which matters because production-grade software demands strict adherence to architectural patterns, databases, and ORMs—not just functionally correct code. The findings could influence future research and tool development for AI-assisted software engineering. The study uses a dual evaluation with end-to-end behavioral tests and static verifiers, and finds framework sensitivity: mid-tier models succeed in minimal, explicit frameworks like Flask but perform substantially worse in convention-heavy environments such as FastAPI and Django. Error analysis identifies data-layer defects—incorrect query composition and ORM runtime violations—as the leading root causes.

Structural constraints include architectural patterns, database schemas, and object-relational mappings (ORMs), which are tools that let developers interact with databases using their programming language instead of raw SQL. This paper isolates the effect of structural complexity by fixing a unified API contract across tasks and frameworks.

LiteMedCoT-VL introduces a LoRA-based fine-tuning pipeline that transfers chain-of-thought reasoning from a 235B teacher model to 2B student models for medical visual question answering. On the PMC-VQA benchmark, it reaches 64.9% accuracy, beating the zero-shot Qwen3-VL-4B baseline of 53.9% by 11.0 percentage points and outperforming all published baselines. All inference is conducted without image captions by default, simulating a physician interpreting a medical image directly without a radiology report, and visual grounding analysis confirms the model relies on image content rather than textual priors. Compact vision-language models of 2-4B parameters can run on resource-constrained hardware but typically lack the multi-step reasoning capacity needed for interpretable clinical decision support. ## [Critical slowing down found in training diffusion models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.12597) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2605.12597v3) analytically shows that training a score-based diffusion model on the O(n) model in the Gaussian large-n limit exhibits critical slowing down in parameter learning, which also degrades generation. The authors find that a two-layer architecture reduces this slowing down, with training time scaling logarithmically rather than quadratically with system size, and a Fourier implementation achieves this without drastically increasing operational complexity. This work bridges statistical field theory and machine learning, offering a rare theoretical handle on when and why diffusion models succeed or fail near criticality. It suggests that the well-known sampling difficulties near phase transitions persist even for learned generative models, but can be mitigated by architectural depth, which could guide the design of more efficient diffusion models for scientific sampling tasks. The analysis is performed in the analytically tractable Gaussian limit n→∞ of the O(n) model, where a one-layer network matching the exact solution exhibits quadratic scaling of training time with system size. Using a two-layer architecture or its Fourier implementation reduces this to logarithmic scaling, though the study is limited to this idealized setting and may not directly generalize to all diffusion model applications.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Diffusion models are generative schemes that learn to reverse a noising process by estimating the score function, and they have become highly effective for image and scientific data generation. The O(n) model is a classic statistical field theory model used to study phase transitions and critical phenomena, where sampling becomes notoriously difficult near criticality due to critical slowing down. Critical slowing down refers to the divergence of relaxation times as a system approaches a critical point, and this paper investigates whether learned samplers inherit the same problem.

<a id="item-150"></a>
## [ConSPO: Contrastive Reformulation Fixes GRPO Limitations in RLVR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.12969) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2605.12969v5) shows that GRPO admits an equivalent discriminative reformulation in which policy optimization maximizes the expected score gap between verified positive and negative rollouts. Building on this analysis, the authors propose ConSPO, a Contrastive Sequence-level Policy Optimization method that uses length-normalized sequence log-probabilities as rollout scores and contrasts verified positives against negative distractors within the same group via a group-wise InfoNCE-style objective with a curriculum-scheduled margin. GRPO is one of the most widely adopted RLVR algorithms for post-training LLMs on reasoning tasks, so identifying its objective-level flaws and offering a drop-in contrastive alternative could directly improve reasoning alignment pipelines used by researchers and practitioners. The work adds a theoretical lens (discriminative/contrastive view) to the RLVR literature, which has largely been empirical since DeepSeek-R1 popularized GRPO. The paper pinpoints two objective-level limitations of GRPO: likelihood-misaligned surrogate scores (clipped ratio-based scores are optimized instead of the sequence likelihoods that actually govern generation) and score-insensitive credit assignment (rollout-level credit does not reflect current score gaps between positive and negative rollouts). ConSPO addresses these by adaptively strengthening updates for poorly separated positives and high-scoring negatives, with a curriculum-scheduled margin that preserves separation pressure as training progresses; experiments across diverse settings show it outperforms strong baselines on challenging reasoning benchmarks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: RLVR (Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards) trains LLMs using automatically checkable reward signals, such as whether a math or code answer is correct, rather than learned reward models. GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization), popularized by DeepSeek-R1, is a PPO-style RLVR algorithm that samples a group of rollouts per prompt and normalizes rewards within the group to compute advantages, avoiding a separate value network. Contrastive learning, as used in InfoNCE, trains representations by pulling positives closer to an anchor and pushing negatives apart, and this paper imports that idea into sequence-level policy optimization.

## [PaCo-VLA Adds Passivity Shield to Vision-Language-Action Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00515) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PaCo-VLA introduces a passivity-shielded compliance prior that treats Vision-Language-Action (VLA) outputs as task-level compliance proposals—semantic bindings, task stages, and admittance schedules—rather than direct motor commands. A high-frequency, proposal-independent passivity shield governs these proposals through energy-tank accounting and boundary checks, and the authors report superior precision over unshielded VLA baselines with zero passivity violations in simulated and real-world connector-insertion experiments. This work addresses a real reliability gap in contact-rich manipulation by decoupling high-level VLA semantic reasoning from low-level passivity-shielded control, which could influence future VLA safety research and enable safer deployment of foundation models in force-sensitive domains. It provides a provably sampled-passive runtime contract at the admittance port, offering a runtime interface for deploying foundation models in contact-rich tasks. The passivity shield is proposal-independent and uses energy-tank accounting and boundary checks to prevent invalid, stale, or unverified model predictions from bypassing low-level contact physics. The decoupled architecture also enables causal evaluation, isolating semantic contributions from geometric shortcuts, and the framework sustains zero passivity violations even under adversarial compliance shifts.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models are multimodal foundation models that integrate vision, language, and actions, directly outputting low-level robot actions from image observations and text instructions. Contact-rich manipulation refers to robot tasks where sustained, precisely modulated physical contact drives success, such as peg-in-hole insertion, surface wiping, and gear meshing. Passivity-based control is a widely used approach for robot manipulators that exploits system properties to ensure inherent robustness against plant uncertainty and disturbance without producing unnecessarily large control effort.

## [AgenticRL Uses Self-Refining Agents to Automate UAV Navigation Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03963) ⭐️ 7.0/10

AgenticRL introduces a multimodal closed-loop framework in which role-specialized agents generate executable reward functions, diagnose the failures of the policies trained with them, and issue targeted refinement instructions to regenerate improved rewards. Evaluated on eight UAV tasks, it reaches 100% success in racing and 88% in cluttered navigation, and reward refinement lifts mean simulation success from 37.2% to 96.4%. Manual reward engineering is a major bottleneck in applying deep reinforcement learning to real robots, so automating both reward generation and behavioral diagnosis could substantially reduce the human effort needed to deploy autonomous UAVs. The reported 90.0% real-world success rate and 93.4% sim-to-real accuracy suggest the approach may transfer beyond simulation, which matters for robotics and RL practitioners alike. Before training, a task grounding stage automatically selects a compatible action profile along with its observation and reward interfaces, and each generated reward is used to train a policy with Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) that is then evaluated under randomized conditions. Diagnosis packets combine task-level behavioral, geometric, and safety measurements with the current reward code, task specification, behavioral summary, and visual scene context, distinguishing AgenticRL from one-shot reward generation, human-guided refinement, or broad candidate search.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Deep reinforcement learning lets robots learn complex navigation tasks, but it typically depends on time-consuming manual reward design and fine-tuning. Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) is a widely used policy gradient algorithm for training such agents, while recent automated reward design methods use large language models or language agents to propose reward functions. AgenticRL builds on this line of work by adding an automated diagnosis loop that directs each subsequent reward revision based on the behavior the previous reward induced.

## [Lightweight Autoregressive Framework Achieves Near Log-Linear Graph Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04287) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2606.04287v2) proposes a lightweight autoregressive framework for graph generation that uses structure-guided topological ordering to serialize graphs into regular edge sequences, enabling near log-linear generation complexity. It also introduces a two-phase training strategy combining exploration-oriented augmentation with iterative refinement to reduce overfitting and promote controlled novelty. Current graph generative models, including diffusion-based and autoregressive approaches, suffer from scalability bottlenecks and tend to imitate training graphs rather than generalize, limiting their usefulness in domains like molecular discovery, circuit design, and cybersecurity. This work addresses both scalability and novelty, potentially enabling more practical and creative graph generation on large-scale real-world problems. The framework supports both LSTM and Mamba-style causal sequence backbones, and experiments on molecular and non-molecular benchmarks show improved novelty while preserving high validity and uniqueness. Large-memory accelerators enable longer graph-sequence experiments beyond typical GPU limits, though the paper does not specify exact complexity constants or dataset sizes.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Graph generation is a core machine learning task with applications in drug design, electronic circuit synthesis, and network security. Diffusion-based models generate graphs by iteratively denoising full adjacency matrices, which is computationally expensive, while many autoregressive models have at least quadratic complexity. Topological ordering is a way to linearize a directed acyclic graph by ordering vertices so that each edge goes from an earlier to a later vertex, which can make sequence-based generation more efficient.

## [WorldRoamBench: A New Benchmark for Long-Horizon Stability of Interactive World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.31672) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced WorldRoamBench, an open-world benchmark that evaluates the long-horizon stability of interactive world models (IWMs) across four dimensions: action, vision, physics, and memory. The benchmark contains over 1,000 test cases spanning Nature, Urban, and Indoor scenes in first- and third-person views, with 10-60 seconds of continuous WASD interaction, and testing 10+ open- and closed-source models revealed that none reliably satisfies all four dimensions. Short-horizon performance has been the standard way to judge interactive world models, but this benchmark shows that such results do not guarantee sustained action following, visual stability, physical plausibility, or memory. By exposing failures that trajectory-level or start-vs-end comparisons miss, WorldRoamBench sets a more rigorous evaluation target that could steer future IWM research toward models that are deployable in real-world applications. Each dimension comes with a tailored metric: a per-frame action metric that bypasses cross-model semantic scale disparity, a sliding-window drift metric that captures non-monotonic mid-sequence collapse, a physics evaluation across mechanics, optics, and 3D consistency gated by camera-motion and subject-tracking checks, and a trajectory-aware memory protocol using transition-localized 3D point-cloud reconstruction and tracking-plus-VLM reasoning. Even the best-performing model achieves only moderate scores, and the benchmark is currently an arXiv preprint without community validation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Interactive world models are AI systems that generate and respond to dynamic environments as a user acts within them, and they are increasingly seen as a foundation for simulation, robotics, and game-like agents. Long-horizon stability means the model keeps behaving consistently over extended interaction rather than degrading after a few seconds. Evaluating this is hard because different models use different action semantics, and failures can be hidden by aggregate trajectory scores or by comparing only the start and end of a sequence. WorldRoamBench addresses these gaps with dimension-specific metrics and a large, diverse test suite.

## [STL-Based Reward Shaping Enables Explicit Quadruped Gait Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00442) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2607.00442v2) introduces a framework that uses parameterized Signal Temporal Logic (STL) specifications to shape rewards for reinforcement learning, enabling explicit control over quadruped gait behaviors such as walking-trot, trot, and bound. The authors define parametric STL templates for three speed regimes, calibrate them from reference rollouts, and compute rewards via smooth approximations of STL robustness, then instantiate the approach on Google's Barkour robot in MuJoCo XLA (MJX) using PPO. This work addresses a known limitation of hand-crafted Markovian rewards in quadruped locomotion, offering improved interpretability and explicit control over gait structures. It could inspire further research in the robotics and RL communities by providing a formal, specification-driven alternative to reward engineering. The framework uses smooth approximations of STL robustness to generate dense, continuous reward landscapes compatible with Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO). Experiments show that compared with hand-crafted rewards, an expert-switching oracle, and Text2Reward, the proposed Human-STL method maintains high command-tracking success across the evaluated speed range while exhibiting substantially higher consistency with intended speed-dependent gait structures.

## [GeoSelect: Training-Free Spatial-Program Execution for Remote Sensing Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03869) ⭐️ 7.0/10

GeoSelect introduces a training-free pipeline that reframes referring remote sensing image segmentation as the execution of a typed spatial program synthesized by a frozen, text-only language model. It achieves 58.86 mIoU on RRSIS-D test and 55.27 mIoU on RISBench test, more than doubling the best prior training-free method on RRSIS-D without any referring supervision and on a single GPU. This work addresses a key limitation of existing training-free methods, which rely on implicit vision-language activations or region-text similarity and thus give weak control over the spatial, superlative, and ordinal relations that dominate aerial referring expressions. By making execution explicit and inspectable, it could inspire similar neuro-symbolic pipelines for other spatial reasoning tasks. The central abstraction is a single scored candidate set type under which every operator composes: continuous geometric fields realize position and proximity, while discrete set and order operators add extremum, ordinal, top-k, and relational constructions that fields alone cannot express. A reliability ladder degrades any failing program to the field-only special case, and diagnostics motivate complementary tests of proposal recall and program-path behavior.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Referring remote sensing image segmentation is the task of segmenting the object named by a natural-language expression in an aerial image, such as 'the rightmost ship' or 'the second court from the left'. Existing training-free approaches resolve the expression through implicit vision-language activations or region-text similarity, which offers weak control over spatial, superlative, and ordinal relations. GeoSelect instead uses a frozen text-only language model to synthesize the expression into a small domain-specific language, which is checked for well-formedness and then run by a deterministic executor.

## [Introspection Threshold Proposed as Prerequisite for LLM Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04277) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2607.04277v2) argues that sustainable recursive self-improvement in Large Language Models requires true introspection, drawing an analogy to von Neumann's complexity threshold for self-reproducing automata. The authors use Kleene's Second Recursion Theorem to show introspective programs theoretically exist, but find that current Transformer-based LLMs only exhibit quasi-introspection due to structural bottlenecks. This work reframes the debate over self-improving AI by identifying introspection as a concrete architectural prerequisite rather than a vague capability, which could guide AI safety research and the design of future model architectures. It matters for researchers and labs pursuing self-evolving AI systems, as it suggests current architectures may be fundamentally incapable of safe, sustainable self-improvement. The paper identifies three structural bottlenecks preventing true introspection: a lack of complete self-access, the feedforward nature of the Transformer, and computational class constraints that prevent fixed-point iteration. It concludes by outlining architectural paths to cross this complexity threshold and discussing associated safety implications.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Kleene's Second Recursion Theorem is a foundational result in computability theory showing that computable functions can be applied to their own descriptions, enabling fixed points and self-referential programs like quines. Von Neumann's complexity threshold refers to his theory of self-reproducing automata, which posits a minimum complexity needed for a machine to reproduce and evolve itself. Transformers are the dominant neural architecture behind modern LLMs, but their feedforward, attention-based design processes each token without recurrent self-modification, which the paper argues limits genuine self-simulation.

## [Prompt-Driven Exploration Uses VLMs to Guide VLA Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08837) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2607.08837v4) introduces Prompt-Driven Exploration (PDE), a reinforcement learning method in which a vision-language model watches rollout videos, diagnoses how the policy behaved, and rewrites the natural language prompt to induce better behavior. The authors report that PDE lets RL learn successful policies even from zero-reward starts and improves sample efficiency across manipulation and reasoning tasks. Standard exploration adds noise in the action space, which only produces rollouts close to the current policy and often fails when rewards are sparse. By perturbing the language prompt instead, PDE enables global behavioral changes that could help VLA-based robots escape weak policies and learn from zero-reward starts, a long-standing obstacle in real-world robotics RL. The method resembles posterior sampling at the level of prompts: the VLM maintains an implicit distribution over useful prompts and updates it from observed rollouts. It is an arXiv preprint with a strong MIT-affiliated author list and a project website, but no peer review or community discussion yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language-action (VLA) models such as OpenVLA condition robot policies on both visual input and natural language instructions, so changing the prompt changes the whole rollout. Reinforcement learning typically explores by injecting noise into actions (e.g., Gaussian noise or epsilon-greedy), but this only yields local variations, and sparse-reward tasks give too little signal to select good prompts. PDE combines these ideas by using a VLM to reason over rollout videos and rewrite prompts, treating language as the exploration space.

A new arXiv paper (2607.21971v2) introduces MetaEvolve, a framework that cultivates self-evolution meta-skills in LLMs through a data synthesis pipeline, evolution-aware reinforcement learning, and inference-time evolutionary search. Grounded in coding tasks, MetaEvolve outperforms the strongest baseline by 10.01% absolute on in-distribution benchmarks and 24.12% on out-of-distribution benchmarks, and achieves a 46.9% relative improvement on open-ended algorithm optimization problems outside the training domain. If such meta-skills transfer broadly, they could improve LLM performance on open-ended problems where rich reward signals are scarce. MetaEvolve synthesizes evolution trajectories as training data, each containing a current program, a fitness score combining correctness and efficiency, and a history of prior attempts, then trains via RL with verifiable rewards derived from test case execution. ## [Instruction Tuning Boosts Model Confidence, Reduces Rationale Diversity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13430) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2608.13430v2) evaluates three matched base and instruction-tuned models across question-answering benchmarks and finds that instruction tuning consistently increases answer confidence even though predictive accuracy barely changes. It also reports that cross-rationale diversity consistently decreases, while surface-level lexical diversity varies in direction and magnitude across models and benchmarks. The findings suggest that instruction tuning can induce verbalized overconfidence and make supporting rationales less varied, which matters for anyone relying on model explanations in high-stakes settings. It highlights a trade-off between the helpfulness of instruction-tuned models and the reliability and diversity of their reasoning traces. The paper reports that instruction tuning degrades likelihood-based calibration, and that the observed differences persist after controlling for answer selection and rationale length, confirming that confidence and rationale diversity capture distinct effects. The effect on surface-level lexical diversity is non-uniform, varying in both direction and magnitude across models and benchmarks.

This paper connects these concepts to study whether instruction tuning's confidence boost comes at the cost of less varied rationales.

## [Survey Maps Multi-Turn Conversational AI from Text to Multimodal Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17605) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv survey (2608.17605v2) reviews multi-turn conversational AI across text-only dialogue, AudioLLMs and speech-native systems, multimodal and omni-modal systems, and tool-augmented agents, organizing the literature by datasets and benchmarks, modeling paradigms, training strategies, evaluation setups, and cross-cutting challenges. The authors conclude that multimodal capability has advanced faster than the ability to sustain coherent interaction across a session, and they propose a research agenda for systems that can remember, revise, ground, speak, listen, act, and adapt across turns, modalities, and cultures. Multi-turn dialogue is where conversational AI meets real user behavior — clarifying goals, revising requests, interrupting, and switching topics — so this synthesis gives AI/ML researchers and practitioners a single structured reference for datasets, benchmarks, and training approaches across text, speech, multimodal, and tool-augmented systems. By explicitly flagging persistent memory, cross-turn grounding, full-duplex interaction, robust evaluation, and cultural alignment as unsolved, it helps direct future research and product development toward the gaps that matter most. The survey spans four system families — text-only dialogue, AudioLLMs and speech-native systems, multimodal/omni-modal systems, and tool-augmented agents — and is accompanied by a companion GitHub repository (faiza-sfa/multiturn-conversational-ai-survey). Its central caveat is that current systems still struggle with persistent memory, cross-turn grounding, full-duplex interaction, robust evaluation, and cultural alignment, meaning stronger perception and generation do not automatically yield coherent long sessions.

## [AI Type Vectors Model Human Choices Across Economic Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18265) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2608.18265v4) introduces a general AI-based modeling technique that assigns a large language model a vector of trait intensities—a 'type vector'—and asks it to make choices, then varies traits and values to minimize distance to human decisions. The method was validated on 119,147 decisions made by 78,657 subjects from more than 35 countries across 10 classic economic game roles, showing human behavior can be closely matched using three dimensions: Risk Aversion, Strategic Sophistication, and Trust. This approach bridges natural language interpretability with fittable mathematical structure, offering a portable, low-dimensional representation of behavior that could advance behavioral economics, AI alignment, and computational social science. It suggests that general yet parsimonious theories of human behavior may be possible across diverse settings. The type vectors needed to fit individuals across games cluster into fewer than a dozen groups, with substantial variation in fit across subjects, and individual type vectors can predict behavior in held-out games with different rules and available actions. The technique is highly generalizable and interpretable, allowing any vector of traits to be input to model behavior across any setting.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models have shown human-like biases in preference tasks involving risk, time discounting, and decision framing, making them useful tools for simulating human behavior. Traditional behavioral economics models often rely on fixed mathematical forms that are hard to interpret, while pure language descriptions lack quantitative fit. This paper combines both by using LLM-assigned numeric trait vectors as a flexible, fittable representation of personality.

## [ESSE: An LLM Tutor That Grades Student Self-Explanations in CS1](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.25180) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces ESSE, an LLM-powered self-explanation tutor for introductory programming in which students explain lines of worked examples and receive immediate LLM feedback on the correctness and completeness of each explanation. The authors evaluated the LLM's judgments against two independent human reference standards — a single domain expert and a crowd of non-expert raters — and deployed the tutor in an introductory Java course, finding that feedback led students to persist and revise rather than abandon a line, with explanations growing more complete and conceptually richer across attempts. Self-explanation is a well-established active-learning strategy, but it has been hard to scale in CS1 because assessing free-text explanations and returning timely feedback had no easy automated solution. If LLM-based assessment is good enough to power such a tutor, it could make active study of worked examples practical in large introductory programming courses and inform broader AI-in-education deployments. The study characterizes both where the LLM is reliable and the systematic tendencies in how it diverges from human judgment, using two reference standards of different kinds — a single domain expert and a crowd of non-expert raters — each with its own strengths and weaknesses. The deployment evidence is behavioral and learning-oriented: students persisted and revised, and their explanations became more complete and conceptually richer across attempts.

The bottleneck has been assessment: judging free-text explanations and giving timely feedback traditionally required human graders, which does not scale. LLM-as-a-judge is an emerging evaluation paradigm in which a language model scores another system's outputs against a rubric, and this paper tests whether that paradigm is reliable enough for a tutoring role.

## [Training-Free Self-Embedding Steganography Defends Against Partial Speech Deepfakes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.25285) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2608.25285v2) proposes a training-free proactive defense against partial deepfake speech, in which a clean speech signal embeds a compressed representation of itself via self-embedding steganography. This allows post-hoc extraction of reference content, enabling detection and codec-based restoration of manipulated segments without any model training. Partial deepfake speech, where only short segments of an utterance are manipulated, is notoriously hard for passive detectors as the spoofed proportion shrinks. A training-free, data-efficient defense that complements passive detection could meaningfully improve audio security and forensic reliability for voice authentication and media verification. The approach repurposes existing audio steganography methods rather than training new models, and experiments on a benchmark dataset show it complements passive defenses. The paper notes that accurate detection and restoration remain challenging as the proportion of spoofed regions decreases, which is the core limitation the method targets.

**Background**: Audio steganography hides information inside an audio signal so it is imperceptible to listeners; self-embedding is a variant in which a compressed copy of the signal is embedded into itself, a technique previously used for image and video tamper detection. Partial deepfake speech refers to utterances where only limited temporal segments are synthesized or edited, making utterance-level classifiers unreliable. Passive defenses detect manipulation after the fact without any watermark, while proactive defenses embed information in advance to enable later verification.

## [PAVE combines predictive learning and value-guided evolution for robot policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30378) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PAVE is a direct world-action policy that adds trajectory-relative multi-horizon transition alignment at 25%, 50%, 75%, and 100% of the remaining episode on top of a local fixed-offset JEPA objective, while training an independent distributional value critic on cumulative deployment trajectories. It converts action-chunk-aligned N-step advantages into positive, negative, or null text conditions for a flow-matching actor, and reports the strongest overall performance across three simulation benchmarks while keeping the direct actor's online execution path. Standard behavior cloning leaves two gaps: representations are not explicitly required to describe multi-timescale scene evolution, and deployment trajectories of unequal quality are reused without separating useful dynamics from undesirable behavior. PAVE addresses both, which could improve how vision-language-action policies learn from mixed-quality robot data in embodied AI. The multi-horizon targets are training-only and do not supply explicit future tokens to the action head, and the multi-horizon predictor and critic are removed from online execution, so deployment still generates actions directly from the current observation, language instruction, and proprioception. The abstract is truncated and no community discussion is available, so independent validation is still limited.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language-action (VLA) policies map camera observations and natural-language instructions to continuous robot actions, and behavior cloning trains them by imitating expert demonstrations as a supervised learning problem. JEPA-style predictive learning trains representations to predict latent targets rather than reconstruct pixels, while flow-matching actors generate actions by transporting a simple distribution to the action distribution. Value critics estimate expected returns and are commonly used in reinforcement learning to distinguish better from worse behavior.

## [SARA Attack Defeats Token Shuffling Privacy in ViT Split Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01232) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces the Spatially Aligned Reconstruction Attack (SARA), a unified pipeline that predicts token positions, restores spatial layout, reconstructs missing embeddings via a feature-space masked autoencoder, and recovers the input image. It shows that token shuffling in ViT split inference provides only apparent privacy, while token reduction offers stronger but still incomplete protection. This work challenges the assumed privacy benefits of token reduction and shuffling in split-inference systems, which are increasingly used to offload ViT computation from edge devices to the cloud. It highlights that transmitted token embeddings retain substantial positional information, potentially exposing sensitive input data to reconstruction attacks. Experiments use ImageNet-1K with ViT-B/16 and MAE-B/16, and consider two token-reduction strategies: random dropping and ToMe merging, plus token shuffling. The authors also propose a lightweight edge-side defense that removes positional embeddings and progressively adapts edge-side transformer blocks through knowledge distillation, substantially reducing SARA's attack performance while preserving downstream task accuracy and requiring no changes to the cloud-side model.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Vision Transformers (ViTs) split an image into fixed-size patches and process them as a sequence of tokens, similar to words in NLP. In split inference, an edge device transmits intermediate token representations to a remote cloud for further processing, which raises privacy concerns because the embeddings may leak information about the original input. Feature inversion attacks attempt to reconstruct the input image from these transmitted embeddings, and token reduction (dropping or merging tokens) and token shuffling are commonly assumed to mitigate such leakage.

## [VLA-Precision: Efficient Real-World Online RL for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04355) ⭐️ 7.0/10

VLA-Precision introduces the Asymmetric Co-Bootstrapping (ACoB) algorithm and the ACoB-Stream architecture to enable efficient real-world online reinforcement learning for vision-language-action models. Evaluated on nine high-precision chemistry tasks across four robot embodiments, it achieves a 98.3% mean success rate in 45.8 minutes per task, with 27.6-second episodes running at 1.2x and 1.8x the speeds of VLA and RL baselines. Real-world online RL post-training lets VLA models improve beyond demonstrations through autonomous trial and error, but unreliable value signals and large-model overhead have limited practical deployment. By addressing policy drift and sample efficiency, VLA-Precision could make precision-critical robotic manipulation in domains like chemistry more reliable and practical. ACoB uses asymmetric co-bootstrapping across timescales: early intervention-guided behavioral learning rapidly improves policy performance and online experience quality, while accumulated autonomous experience drives global return propagation and local preference ranking to calibrate value estimates and suppress drift. ACoB-Stream applies invariant-state decoupling and on-demand streaming, delivering up to 10.9x improvements in throughput and computational efficiency.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language-action (VLA) models are multimodal foundation models that take an image of the robot's surroundings plus a text instruction and directly output low-level robot actions, typically built by fine-tuning a vision-language model on robot trajectory data; the concept was pioneered by Google DeepMind's RT-2 in 2023. While pretrained VLAs enable broad manipulation, they remain unreliable for tasks demanding precision and repeatability, and real-world online RL post-training is one way to close that gap. However, applying online RL to large VLAs exposes two bottlenecks: unreliable value signals that can induce policy drift, and the computational overhead of large models that constrains throughput and sample efficiency.

## [Intentest Uses Fact-Intent DAG to Keep LLM Pentest Agents on Track](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07344) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.07344v2) proposes Intentest, an intent-graph-guided LLM agent that externalizes long-horizon state from the model's context window onto a persistent fact-intent directed acyclic graph (DAG), where verified network states become immutable fact nodes and exploration directions become intent edges bounded by predecessor facts. On a benchmark of real CTF challenges spanning more than ten vulnerability types across three difficulty levels, Intentest achieves an overall success rate of 88.2% and 75.0% on hard tasks, improving over the baseline by roughly 44 and 50 percentage points. Long-horizon LLM agents are increasingly applied to cybersecurity tasks such as vulnerability discovery and automated penetration testing, but they suffer from context forgetting and intent drift, where early critical facts and causal reasoning chains are lost and the agent falls into aimless, repetitive exploration. By externalizing state into a structured DAG, Intentest points toward a general recipe for making LLM agents reliable on long-tail, multi-step security tasks, which could affect both AI-driven security tooling and the broader agent-design community. Intentest adopts a three-layer architecture: a fact-intent mapping layer that maintains global state, a task scheduling and allocation layer that ensures execution stability via two-phase degradation recovery and multi-dimensional adaptive load balancing, and an intent retrieval and prediction layer that supplies tactical priors through a top-down five-stage filtering algorithm. Ablation experiments show that intent retrieval and prediction cut the average number of rounds on successful medium and hard tasks by about 33% and 48% respectively, without changing the set of solvable tasks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Automated penetration testing is a representative long-tail security task in which an agent proactively identifies and exploits vulnerabilities, and LLM-based agents have recently been extended into this adversarial domain. A directed acyclic graph (DAG) is a graph with directed edges and no cycles, commonly used to represent dependencies or state transitions; here it stores verified facts and the intents that may follow from them. Context forgetting and intent drift are well-documented failure modes of long-horizon LLM agents, where the model's output gradually diverges from its original goal over extended interactions.

The paper introduces an efficient pipeline that curates a production-ready European Portuguese (PT-PT) corpus of 41 billion tokens from 411 TB of raw data archived by Arquivo.pt. A novel post-scraping block that removes boilerplate and line duplicates before filtering increases final document yield by 19.04% by rescuing valid text that standard heuristic filters would otherwise discard. European Portuguese is a low-resource variant that is easily drowned out by Brazilian Portuguese (PT-BR) in web data, so a clean, large-scale PT-PT corpus directly addresses a key bottleneck for LLM pre-training and multilingual NLP. The 19.04% yield improvement is a reusable technical contribution that could benefit corpus curation for other regional language variants. The pipeline integrates rigorous language identification, weighted fuzzy deduplication, and neural quality classification, and is built on 411 TB of raw data from Arquivo.pt. The key caveat is that the corpus targets only the European Portuguese variant, and its quality depends on the coverage and biases of the underlying web archive.

Web corpora for LLM pre-training are typically built by scraping such archives and then applying language identification, deduplication, and quality filtering to remove noise. Regional variants like PT-PT are especially hard to curate because they share most vocabulary and syntax with a much larger variant, PT-BR, making language identification error-prone.

## [SinkProbe Tests Whether Gated Attention Fixes Attention Sinks at 1M Tokens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08574) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.08574v2) introduces SinkProbe, a diagnostic suite that measures sink mass, massive activation, position-resolved recall, and the recency gap, and applies it to four small models that differ only in how they mix tokens and depth. The study reports three findings: the training objective rather than the architecture produces the attention sink, gated attention did not reproduce its published effect (cutting first-token attention from 46.7% to 4.8% at NeurIPS 2025) at the tested scale, and sink mass, activations, and position bias moved independently. Long-context models now advertise one-million-token windows, but attention sinks and position bias limit how much of that window is actually used, so this work questions whether architectural fixes like gated attention genuinely scale. If the sink is driven by the training objective rather than architecture, the long-context community may need to rethink both model design and evaluation protocols for million-token contexts. The paper tests only four small models, so the failure to reproduce gated attention's published effect may be scale-dependent, and the work is a preprint that has not undergone peer review. SinkProbe's code, data, and measurement protocol are released at the project's GitHub repository, and the diagnostics are applied at a one-million-token window, eight times beyond the range where such measurements have previously been reported.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Attention sinks are a well-documented phenomenon in which language models assign significant attention to the first token even when it is not semantically important, and they matter because windowed attention models can lose fluency once the first token leaves the context window. Gated attention uses trainable, context-conditioned multiplicative gates to modulate attention distributions, and Kimi K3 combines this idea with Kimi Delta Attention (KDA), a linear attention module extending Gated DeltaNet with finer-grained gating, and Attention Residuals behind a one-million-token window. This paper asks whether such architectural fixes survive the jump to that scale.

## [New bounds show linear accessibility scales linearly, not quadratically](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09556) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.09556v2) derives high-probability bounds for linear accessibility in feature superposition by framing it as a compressed sensing problem under subgaussian noise, proving the sufficient dimension scales as d = O_ε(k log m) rather than the prior worst-case quadratic limit. It also introduces IHT-SAE, a learned iterative refinement method that improves feature recovery beyond the limits of linear availability. This result tightens the theoretical understanding of the linear representation hypothesis, which underpins much of mechanistic interpretability and sparse autoencoder research. It offers a principled framework for evaluating SAEs, compositional generalization, and neural interpretability, potentially guiding more efficient model design and analysis. The analysis characterizes the asymmetry between active and inactive feature interference and the trade-off between interference and observation-noise budgets, validating the bounds through Gaussian-tail approximations. IHT-SAE uses learned iterative refinement to push feature recovery past the linear accessibility limit, though the work remains a preprint without community discussion yet.

**Background**: Feature superposition refers to a neural network's ability to encode more concepts than it has dimensions, but cross-feature interference limits how many simultaneously active features can be linearly read out. Compressed sensing is a signal processing framework for recovering sparse signals from limited linear measurements, often using ℓ1-minimization. Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) are a popular tool in mechanistic interpretability for decomposing neural activations into interpretable features, and their performance is closely tied to these accessibility limits.

## [Data-free On-policy Distillation: Eight Prompts Match 17k Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14193) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.14193v2) shows that on-policy distillation (OPD) is almost indifferent to training data: eight prompts already match a 17k-problem dataset, and three independently built datasets with several-fold differences in difficulty and teacher-student KL produce nearly indistinguishable training curves. The authors push this to the limit with Data-free On-policy Distillation (DF-OPD), where the teacher writes its own training questions under a simple prompt with no external data or quality filtering, matching or surpassing real data and closing 98.5% of the headroom in multi-teacher distillation with just 1k self-generated questions. This finding challenges the assumption that large, carefully curated datasets are essential for on-policy distillation, a standard component of frontier post-training pipelines, and could substantially reduce data collection and filtering costs. It suggests OPD transfers the teacher's mode of reasoning rather than domain-specific knowledge, which has practical implications for multi-teacher distillation where the (prompt, domain) pairs are often unavailable. The paper attributes the data-agnostic behavior to two causes: the unit of data in OPD is the state a prompt leads to rather than the prompt itself, so a single prompt keeps exposing new teacher corrections as sampling continues while the marginal value of additional prompts collapses after eight; and replacing mathematics with competitive programming still recovers over ninety percent of the in-domain gain. DF-OPD's self-generated questions track the teacher's own post-training data on three key diagnostics of training dynamics, which other real datasets do not.

**Background**: On-policy distillation (OPD) is a post-training technique in which a student language model generates its own outputs and is trained to match the teacher's token-level probability distribution, typically via KL divergence, rather than imitating a fixed offline dataset. It has become a standard part of frontier post-training pipelines for transferring capabilities from large teacher models into smaller, deployable students. A new arXiv review paper (arXiv:2609.14819v2) outlines key principles and methods for evaluating large language models in healthcare, covering four areas: study design, statistical methods, capability evaluation, and clinical context evaluation. It emphasizes that LLM evaluation is harder than traditional machine learning due to probabilistic, open-ended outputs and behavior that shifts with prompt design and accumulated context. As LLMs are increasingly deployed in medicine, rigorous evaluation is essential to ensure they provide benefit rather than harm, and this review fills a gap by offering structured, pragmatic guidance for researchers and practitioners. It is likely to become a widely referenced resource for designing and executing healthcare LLM evaluations. The paper distinguishes capability evaluation—covering multiple-choice, agentic, and multi-turn benchmarks plus operational metrics like token usage—from clinical context evaluation, which addresses establishing accuracy of free-text outputs through methods such as human review, LLM-as-a-judge, and clinical trial approaches. A recurring theme is that evaluation methods must be aligned with the specific research question, and the authors describe underlying concepts and potential pitfalls throughout.

## [PAIR Companion Agent Sustains Emotional Support Over 14 Days](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16344) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers deployed PAIR, a theory-based emotion-regulation companion agent, with 19 participants over 14 days, collecting 1,093 sessions that paired emotion estimates with self-reports before and after guided conversations. The study found that guided conversations were followed by higher emotional valence and state-dependent arousal changes, and that perceived helpfulness of the guided conversation significantly increased over time. This is one of the few longitudinal deployments showing that generative companion agents can provide sustained, rather than merely momentary, emotional support, which matters for the design of long-term AI companions in mental health and well-being contexts. The finding that perceived helpfulness grows over time suggests that memory and personalization mechanisms, not just single-turn empathy, drive user-perceived value. The agent's emotion estimates matched self-reported valence and dominance more closely than arousal, and cross-session personalization was linked to memory updates and retained corrections. The study is limited to 19 participants over 14 days, so generalization to broader or clinical populations remains untested.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Emotion regulation theory describes how people consciously monitor and modify their emotional responses, and generative agents are AI systems that can hold open-ended conversations and maintain memory across sessions. Prior work showed such agents can offer momentary emotional support, but whether that support holds up over repeated, real-world use was unclear. PAIR is a companion agent built on emotion-regulation theory that guides users through reflective conversations and adapts based on prior interactions.

<a id="item-175"></a>
## [PentestChain: Zero-Cost Automated Pentesting via MCP and Free-Tier LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18120) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PentestChain is a ten-phase automated penetration testing framework that pairs a deterministic exploit map with a cost-aware AI cascade: a local Ollama qwen2.5-7b model first, then free-tier OpenRouter and Cerebras, with a rule-based fallback that always produces output. It exposes the full pipeline through a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server with eleven tools, and reports zero measured paid-API cost while detecting 26 services and enriching 34 CVEs on legacy targets. Premium frontier models like GPT-4 make continuous automated penetration testing unaffordable for the smaller organizations that need it most, so a zero-paid-cost framework could democratize security testing. The paper also treats US-dollar cost per engagement as a first-class evaluation metric and analyzes the attack surface that an MCP-exposed offensive engine introduces. The framework grounds a four-position threat model in the 2025 MCP incident record, including the CVE-2025-6514 remote-code-execution flaw in mcp-remote, the postmark-mcp supply-chain backdoor, and the tool-poisoning/rug-pull/line-jumping class, and contributes four mitigations. Evaluation uses a reproducible containerized protocol aligned with AutoPenBench, a Cybench subset, and the PentestGPT 182-sub-task benchmark, with more than 10 trials per configuration, pass-at-k, non-parametric significance tests, effect sizes, and direct same-testbed reproduction of the PentestGPT and PentestAgent baselines.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Ollama is a tool for running large language models locally, allowing inference without sending data to cloud APIs. OpenRouter is a unified API gateway that routes requests to models from many providers and offers a free tier with rate limits, while Cerebras provides fast inference hardware and free-tier access. The Model Context Protocol (MCP) is a standard that lets AI models call external tools and services, which is powerful for automation but also expands the attack surface of the exposed engine.

## [CPR framework combines composing, performing, and refining for piano rendering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18216) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new paper introduces the Composer-Performer-Refiner (CPR) framework, a continuous autoregressive model for prompt-conditioned piano MIDI-to-Music rendering. Composer predicts continuous hidden states, Performer generates 24kHz acoustic latents via local flow matching, and Refiner upsamples the waveform to 48kHz, with Bottlenecked Representation Alignment (BREPA) and Modality-Time RoPE (MT-RoPE) strengthening semantics and temporal alignment. CPR addresses the trade-off between discrete-codec autoregressive models, which lose acoustic detail through quantization, and flow matching models, which incur full-sequence attention costs and weaker semantic structure. By operating directly on continuous representations, it could improve both quality and efficiency of expressive piano rendering, benefiting music generation and audio synthesis research. The framework generates 24kHz acoustic latents through local flow matching and upsamples to 48kHz, bypassing the quantization bottleneck while lowering computational costs compared to full-sequence attention. Code is available at the official GitHub repository, though the abstract does not report quantitative benchmarks or comparisons against baselines.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Piano MIDI-to-Music rendering aims to synthesize realistic piano audio from symbolic note sequences while matching the timbre of a reference recording. Autoregressive models generate outputs step by step with causal temporal modeling but often rely on discrete codecs that discard acoustic detail, while flow matching and diffusion models preserve acoustic structure but require costly full-sequence attention. Continuous autoregressive models combine these strengths by predicting continuous representations directly, avoiding quantization loss.

## [Graph-RAG Beats Standard RAG on Cultural QA Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18317) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper benchmarks Graph-RAG against standard RAG on LatamQA, a culturally grounded multiple-choice dataset covering eight thematic categories, with graphs built end-to-end from Wikipedia using the KGGen extractor and no manual curation. Using G-Retriever, Graph-RAG reduced the base LLM's error by 72% with a standard knowledge graph and 78% with a benchmark-aware variant, and the trained projection transferred zero-shot to Portuguese without target-language fine-tuning. This work targets the long-tail deficit of LLMs on culturally specific facts from underrepresented regions such as Latin America, showing that structured knowledge graphs can substantially cut errors compared with text-based retrieval. If the results generalize, Graph-RAG could improve explainability and updatability of retrieval systems and extend multilingual coverage to low-resource languages without extra fine-tuning. The main setting builds graphs automatically from Wikipedia articles with KGGen, avoiding manual curation, while the benchmark-aware variant orients the graph toward task-relevant content to narrow the gap with RAG. G-Retriever combines graph neural networks, LLMs, and RAG, and the zero-shot Portuguese transfer suggests the learned projection is not language-specific.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models often fail on culturally specific facts from underrepresented regions because such facts appear too rarely in pretraining data, a problem known as the long-tail deficit. Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) mitigates this by grounding generation in external text, while Graph-RAG instead retrieves from knowledge graphs—structured models of entities and relationships—offering tighter control over context and better explainability. G-Retriever is a model that integrates graph neural networks, LLMs, and RAG for question answering over textual graphs, and KGGen is a recent open-domain extractor used here to build graphs automatically.

## [ASLEval Exposes Privacy Leakage Gaps in LLM Agent Sessions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18864) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.18864v2) introduces ASLEval, an authorization-aware framework that measures 'privacy exposure displacement' — the mismatch between local evaluation proxies and target-grounded session exposure in tool-using LLM agents. Across multiple enterprise-style environments and independently implemented runtimes, the authors found that an expected-outlet-only view misses 46.9% of exposure recovered by the union of all declared visible exits. The findings suggest that current privacy evaluations of LLM agents, which typically inspect only a designated action, final response, or attacker report, can systematically undercount unauthorized data exposure. This has direct implications for AI safety and security benchmarking, since enterprises deploying tool-using agents may be relying on incomplete evidence when assessing privacy risk. The paper reports three recurring patterns: attacker self-reports combine omissions with high false discovery; schema-aligned internal evidence usually precedes visible exposure at the request/probe level; and reducing model-visible returns can change this path but may eliminate normal-task success. Independent human review supported the adjudication pipeline while flagging harder console and candidate cases.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Tool-using LLM agents operate in multi-step sessions where they call external tools, read data, and produce responses, creating many potential points where sensitive information could leak. Existing privacy evaluations often rely on 'local proxies' — checking a single action, final answer, or attacker report — rather than auditing the entire session. ASLEval addresses this by pre-registering a hidden target set, measuring all declared visible exits, and reserving internal traces for diagnosis, with the goal of grounding privacy claims in explicit authorization and reporting privacy alongside task utility.

## [CoReLoop Boosts Audio Deepfake Detection with Lightweight Recurrent Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19818) ⭐️ 7.0/10

CoReLoop introduces lightweight recurrent refinement modules and loop-specific low-rank adapters that adapt recurrent inputs to a frozen SSL-based audio deepfake detector, reducing pooled cross-domain EER from 4.85% to 3.74% across 14 test sets with only about 10M trainable parameters out of 598M. An optional halting head further selects refinement depth per utterance, achieving 3.73% pooled EER with an average of 1.18 passes. This work shows that a frozen self-supervised audio deepfake detector can be significantly improved for unseen attacks without additional data or modifying its original weights, which is valuable because collecting training data covering all potential attacks is impractical. The parameter-efficient design also lowers the cost of adapting large detection models in real-world deployment. The method trains only lightweight refinement modules and loop-specific low-rank adapters on the original data, preserving the detector's original first-pass prediction; the 24-layer model uses two passes to reach 3.74% pooled EER, and the halting head reduces the average passes to 1.18. A diagnostic showed that directly recycling encoder outputs as inputs degrades detection, motivating the controlled adaptation of recurrent inputs and state updates.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Audio deepfake detection aims to distinguish synthetic or manipulated speech from genuine recordings, and models are typically evaluated by equal error rate (EER), the threshold where false positive and false negative rates are equal. Self-supervised learning (SSL) encoders pretrained on large speech corpora provide strong representations, but detectors built on them often fail to generalize to unseen attack types. Parameter-efficient fine-tuning methods such as low-rank adapters (LoRA) add small trainable matrices to a frozen backbone, enabling adaptation with far fewer parameters.

## [PACE Compiles Screenplays into Precise Diffusion Prompts and 3D Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19853) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PACE (Precise AI Cinematic Expression) introduces a typed, hierarchical representation that captures screenplay evidence, characters, props, locations, subject positions, and camera behavior, with each value written once at its proper level (script, scene, shot, or panel) and inherited below. A compiler converts this representation into both a diffusion-model prompt and a metric 3D scene, while a camera solver places the camera so the declared framing matches the built framing, with code released at github.com/StudioPiLabs/pace-core. This work targets a core weakness in AI film generation: free-text prompts let diffusion models decide spatial layout by their own defaults, so framing and staging are hard to control or measure. By making cinematic plans typed, compilable, and measurable, PACE could give AI-assisted filmmaking a more deterministic and auditable pipeline from screenplay to shot. On the 11-scene Automatic Drive screenplay, every staged single-subject panel places its subject within 1.2% of frame width of the declared position, but with two or three subjects a single camera pose cannot satisfy every position and the residual is reported rather than absorbed. On 204 external director-storyboard shots, delivered head height is 1.906 times the staged target from the director's words, 1.733 from the compiled prompt, and 0.955 with greybox control, and declaring the pose on 30 shots raises the action drawn from 58.9% to 74.4% without moving the framing.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Diffusion models generate images from text prompts but have no explicit notion of where subjects stand or what a camera sees, so spatial planning is left to the model's defaults. PACE treats a screenplay as source code for a cinematic plan: a typed representation is compiled into a prompt and a 3D scene, and a camera solver computes the camera pose that realizes the declared framing. This is an arXiv preprint (2609.19853v2) and has not yet undergone peer review or community validation.

## [LoRA-Adapted DINOv3 ViT for Synthetic Aperture Sonar Target Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21061) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.21061) adapts DINOv3 Vision Transformers to underwater synthetic aperture sonar (SAS) automatic target recognition using a three-stage parameter-efficient framework combining LoRA, hard-negative mining, and supervised contrastive learning. LoRA alone raised AUPRC from 0.300 to 0.679 ± 0.027 with the backbone frozen, while the two refinement stages produced statistically null results. The paper shows that a single parameter-efficient adaptation stage can bridge the gap between natural-image pretraining and underwater acoustic data, training only 0.26% of weights at rank 4. Its negative results on stacked refinement stages offer a useful caution for practitioners who assume that adding hard-negative mining or contrastive objectives on top of LoRA will always help in specialized sensing domains. Evaluation used at-sea SAS data with a mission-level geographic split, comparisons at 85% test recall, and three random seeds. Hard-negative mining changed AUPRC by -0.0045 ± 0.0119 versus an equal-size random curriculum, and SupCon changed it by +0.0002 ± 0.0096 versus the preceding stage, suggesting the encoder had already fit the mined data and that supervised stages had already imposed most target-clutter geometry.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Synthetic aperture sonar (SAS) is an underwater imaging technique used for naval mine detection and target recognition, but deep learning on SAS is hampered by scarce labeled imagery and heavy background clutter. DINOv3 is Meta's self-supervised vision foundation model built on a Vision Transformer backbone, and LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning method that adds small low-rank matrices to a frozen model instead of retraining all weights. Supervised contrastive learning (SupCon) shapes representations by pulling same-class examples together and pushing different-class examples apart.

## [Ability-Residual Decoupling Cuts Affect Contamination in Cognitive Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21214) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.21214) proposes an ability-residual decoupled framework for affective cognitive diagnosis that explicitly models stable cognitive residuals through student, item, concept, student-concept, and low-rank student-item components before letting an affective module modulate guess/slip effects. A Q-matrix-constrained concept residual attention mechanism aggregates only item-relevant concept residuals, and experiments on ASSIST2017, ASSIST2012, ASSIST2009, and Junyi across six cognitive diagnosis backbones show response-prediction gains and generally improved affect alignment. The work challenges a common assumption in affective cognitive diagnosis that any residual error left by the cognitive backbone is mainly attributable to affect, showing instead that item calibration bias, concept bias, and student-item matching can form stable cognitive residuals that leak into affective representations. For researchers in educational data mining and AI in education, this offers a more robust way to separate cognitive and affective signals, which could improve knowledge tracing, personalized feedback, and student modeling systems. The framework decomposes residuals into student, item, concept, student-concept, and low-rank student-item components, and uses a Q-matrix-constrained attention mechanism to aggregate only item-relevant concept residuals before the affective branch modulates guess and slip. Validation includes ablation studies, leakage probes, PCA visualization, long-tail analysis, and case studies, though the paper reports results only on four ASSIST/Junyi datasets and requires affect labels for the affect-alignment evaluation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: Cognitive diagnosis infers students' mastery of concepts from their response logs, and is widely used in intelligent education systems for personalized feedback. Traditional approaches such as item response theory (IRT) and cognitive diagnostic models (CDMs) model the interaction between student ability and item characteristics, while affective cognitive diagnosis extends this by incorporating non-cognitive factors like emotion, engagement, and fatigue. A Q-matrix specifies which concepts each item measures, and guess/slip parameters capture the probability of answering correctly without mastery or incorrectly despite mastery.

## [LEGIT: A Credentialing Protocol for Trustworthy AI Agent Marketplaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21325) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.21325v1) introduces LEGIT, a credentialing protocol that connects certification, reputation, and marketplace allocation for AI agent marketplaces. Certification binds measured quality and cost per solved task to an agent configuration, task domain, evaluation budget, and evidence via a signed record, while reputation links past task outcomes to the same identity. As agentic marketplaces emerge where AI agents autonomously complete specialized tasks for buyers, buyers currently cannot easily verify or compare reported benchmark scores across tasks, software, and budgets. LEGIT's approach could enable more trustworthy allocation of tasks and help establish trust infrastructure for the growing AI agent economy. Evaluations reveal cost differences between agent configurations with similar observed task success, and show that comparisons depend on the evaluation budget, supporting the need to bind performance measurements to the tested configuration and resource limits. A complementary analysis quantifies the deposits and fees required to deter reputation manipulation under a stated Sybil attack model.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 21, 04:00

**Background**: AI agent marketplaces are platforms where autonomous AI agents with varying capabilities complete specialized tasks for buyers. A key challenge is that buyers cannot easily determine which agent will perform best on their tasks, since reported benchmark scores may be hard to verify or compare across different tasks, software, and budgets. Credentialing protocols aim to solve this by creating verifiable, signed records of agent performance and reputation.
